Dependence and Independence for Reversible Process Calculi
Clément Aubert, Iain Phillips, Irek Ulidowski

Abstract
To refine formal methods for concurrent systems, there are several ways of enriching classical operational semantics of process calculi. One can enable the auditing and undoing of past synchronisations thanks to communication keys, thus easing the study of true concurrency as a by-product. Alternatively, proof labels embed information about the origins of actions in transition labels, facilitating syntactic analysis. Enriching proof labels with keys enables a theory of the relations on transitions and on events based on their labels only. We offer for the first time separate definitions of dependence relation and independence relation, and prove their complementarity on connected transitions instead of postulating it. Leveraging the recent axiomatic approach to reversibility, we prove the canonicity of these relations and provide additional tools to study the relationships between e.g., concurrency and causality on transitions and events. Finally, we make precise the subtle relationship between bisimulations based on both forward and backward transitions, on key ordering, and on dependency preservation, providing a direct definition of History Preserving bisimulation for CCS.

1
Introduction

The Calculus of Communicating Systems (
\mathsf{CCS}

[
33
]
) is the main starting point
to develop formal methods for concurrent systems, such as the applied
\pi
-calculus
[
1
,
8
]
, that can be used to e.g., verify communication protocols
[
12
,
40
,
26
]
.
At the heart of this formalism lie three core concepts: synchronisation, concurrency of events (or independence of actions), and bisimulation.
In a nutshell, this paper offers an original and definitive answer to the question of defining dependence and independence for a reversible extension of
\mathsf{CCS}
that uses keys to handle synchronisation, and defines a key-based bisimulation relation that is proven to coincide with another new bisimulation defined in terms of (in)dependence.

The starting point is the formal treatment of parallel communications, as improved by
communication keys
:
\mathsf{CCS}
with keys (
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}

[
36
]
) enables the representation of reversible systems
[
3
]
, provides the capacity of auditing (and of undoing) previous synchronisations, and proved useful to study true concurrency
[
35
]
.
Orthogonally, independence allows to disentangle complex situations and to understand the roots of certain decisions or bugs thanks to the dual notion of dependence.
However, those latter notions are not always easy to work with because they are sometimes
defined
by complementarity
[
5
,
21
]
, or defined only on coinitial or composable transitions. While it has been suspected that the order on keys
[
30
]
or the dependence on previous events
[
7
]
could be fruitful in designing bisimulations, no definitive answer had been provided thus far—in part because dependence and independence were difficult to manipulate, and because not leveraging the axiomatic approach to reversibility made reasoning cumbersome and lengthy.

The paper is organised as follows: we first recall how
\mathsf{CCS}
is extended with the so-called proof labels and communication keys to give
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
.
A fresh look is then offered on the notions of dependence and independence for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
by
defining them
separately and then proving their complementarity on connected transitions (e.g., transitions that can be reached from the same process).
We then present the axiomatic approach to reversibility
[
31
,
32
]
and leverage it to establish relevant properties about our semantics for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
.
After briefly discussing why order on keys and dependence are
“the right notions”
to define History Preserving-like bisimulations, we introduce two new bisimulations that preserve key ordering and dependencies, and prove that they coincide for standard
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes.

2
Background and Related Work

The first use of proof labels to represent the concurrency relation between CCS transitions is due to Boudol and Castellani
[
14
,
15
]
.
Their definition of concurrent transitions applied only to coinitial transitions, but was proven
[
21
]
to coincide with causal semantics for
\pi
-calculus
[
23
]
and for causal trees
[
20
]
.
This universality and simplicity made proof labels convenient, but other approaches were designed to capture (in)dependence on composable transitions.
State- and pomset-based semantics of
\mathsf{CCS}
leverage original definitions of independence
[
22
, Definition 3.4]
and dependence
[
18
, p. 952]
, respectively, on composable transitions. Static
[
2
]
and dynamic localities
[
16
]
were developed to capture local causality and concurrency on composable transitions, but required to use occurrence transition systems
[
18
, Sect. 2.6.1]
.
The first correctness criterion relating independence on coinitial and composable transitions was formulated in the reversible setting
[
5
]
and no criterion demanding to prove the complementarity of dependence and independence relations defined separately was, to our knowledge, formulated before.
While a set of belief and mappings supported the canonicity of those notions, to our knowledge they were never proven unique under mild assumptions.

While locality-based equivalences are defined on syntactical models
[
17
,
2
,
16
]
, causal equivalences were formalised mostly for semantic models: History Preserving (HP) bisimulation—slightly coarser than Hereditary HP bisimulation
[
11
,
27
]
—originated on behaviour structures
[
39
]
, and has been reformulated on Petri nets
[
44
]
, causal trees
[
20
]
, process graphs
[
42
]
, configuration structures
[
43
,
37
]
, modal logics
[
10
,
38
]
, and even
automata
[
24
]
.
In the setting of reversible process calculi, HP has been studied in different forms
[
36
,
35
,
6
,
30
]
, some of which syntactical.
A purely syntactical
“local”
HP bisimulation has been defined on
\mathsf{CCS}
processes
[
17
, Sect. A.2]
, but, to our knowledge, the usual HP—e.g., that considers the global causal ordering—was never defined on
\mathsf{CCS}
.
However, it was known that a strong version of causal bisimulation
[
29
, Definition 4.1]
coincided with HP bisimulation
[
20
, Theorem 2.1]
, but it required to use conflict labelled event structures.

In conclusion, process algebra fell short on defining dependence and independence on all combinations of transitions (coinitial and composable), while causal equivalences were expressed on models that required to map back-and-forth notions of cause and concurrency.

3
CCSK with Proof Labels

This section recalls the extension of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
with proof labels (
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
)
[
4
,
5
]
, an LTS in bijection with
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
as discussed in
Appendix A
.

Definition 1

((Co-)names, labels and keys)
.

Let
\mathsf{N}
be a set of
names
, ranged over by
a
,
b
and
c
. A bijection
\overline{\cdot}:\mathsf{N}\to\overline{\mathsf{N}}
, whose inverse is also written
\overline{\cdot}
,
gives the
complement
of a name. The set of
labels

\mathsf{L}
is
\mathsf{N}\cup\overline{\mathsf{N}}\cup\{\tau\}
, and we use
\alpha
,
\beta
(resp.
\lambda
) to range over
\mathsf{L}
(resp.
\mathsf{L}\backslash\{\tau\}
).
It is convenient to extend the complement mapping to labels by letting
\overline{\tau}=\tau
.

Let
\mathsf{K}
be a denumerable set of
keys
, ranged over by
k
,
m
and
n
.

Keyed labels
, denoted
a[k]
,
a[m]
,
a[n]
,
b[k]
, etc., are elements of
\mathsf{L}\times\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{K}}
.

Definition 2

(Operators)
.

The set
\mathbb{X}
of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
processes is defined as usual:

\displaystyle X,Y\coloneqq~

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}

(Inactive process)

\displaystyle\|~

\displaystyle\alpha.X

(Prefix)

\displaystyle\|~

\displaystyle X\backslash\lambda

(Restriction)

\displaystyle\|~

\displaystyle X+Y

(Sum)

\displaystyle\|~

\displaystyle X\mid Y

(Parallel composition)

\displaystyle\|~

\displaystyle\alpha[k].X

(Keyed prefix)

As usual, the inactive process
\mathbf{0}
is omitted when preceded by a (keyed) prefix, and the binding power of the operators
[
33
, p. 68]
, from highest to lowest, is

\backslash\lambda
,
\alpha[k].

\alpha.
,
\mid
and
+
.

We write
\keysop(X)
for the set of keys in
X
. The set of
\mathsf{CCS}
processes is denoted
\mathbb{P}=\{X\mid\keysop(X)=\emptyset\}
, and we let
P
,
Q
range over it.

Definition 3

(Proof labels)
.

We let
\mathrm{d}
range over the
directions

\mathrm{L}
(eft) and
\mathrm{R}
(ight),
\upsilon
,
\upsilon_{1}
and
\upsilon_{2}
range over strings in
\{{\mid_{\mathrm{d}}},{+_{\mathrm{d}}}\}^{*}
, and
\theta
range over
proof keyed labels
:

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\coloneqq\upsilon\alpha[k]~\|~\upsilon\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\upsilon_{1}\lambda[k],{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\upsilon_{2}\overline{\lambda}[k]\rangle

We write
\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
for the set of proof keyed labels, define
\ell:\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{L}
and
\kayop:\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{K}
as

\displaystyle\ell(\upsilon\alpha[k])

\displaystyle=\alpha

\displaystyle\ell(\upsilon\langle\upsilon_{1}\lambda[k],\upsilon_{2}\overline{\lambda}[k]\rangle)

\displaystyle=\tau

\displaystyle\kayop(\upsilon\alpha[k])

\displaystyle=k

\displaystyle\kayop(\upsilon\langle\upsilon_{1}\lambda[k],\upsilon_{2}\overline{\lambda}[k]\rangle)

\displaystyle=k

Notation 3
.

We sometimes write
\theta
with
\kayop(\theta)=k
as
\theta[k]
. We let
\overline{\mathrm{d}}=\mathrm{R}
if
\mathrm{d}=\mathrm{L}
, else
\overline{\mathrm{d}}=\mathrm{L}
.
We generally omit
“keyed”
and simply write
“proof label”
.

Definition 4

(LTS for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
with proof labels
[
4
,
5
]
)
.

The
labelled transition system (LTS) for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
with proof labels
, denoted by
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
, is

Action, Prefix and Restriction

Forward
Äpgjy

\keysop(X)=\emptyset

act

\alpha.X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}}}\alpha[k].X

\kayop(\theta)\neq k

pre

X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}X^{\prime}
\alpha[k].X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}\alpha[k].X^{\prime}

\lablop(\theta)\notin\{a,\overline{a}\}

res

X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}X^{\prime}
X\backslash a\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}X^{\prime}\backslash a

Forward and backward transition rules for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
(
{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}
,

,
{+_{\mathrm{R}}}
and
{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to15.30548pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to15.30548pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to12.70833pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to11.70839pt{}}}$\hss}}}+_{\mathrm{R}}}
omitted).

We note that the LTS for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
can be obtained from
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
by replacing
\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
with
\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{K}}
and
\theta

with
\ell(\theta)[\kayop(\theta)]
in
Figure 1
, which corresponds to erasing the
“proved”
part of labels.
In the following, we write
{\cdot}^{\circ}={({\cdot}^{\dagger}})^{-1}
the bijection between transitions in
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}

[
4
, Lemma 1]
, given in detail in
Appendix A
.

Definition 5

(Transitions)
.

A
transition

t:X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\mkern-5.0mu\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta[k]\mkern 2.0mu$}}$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}}}}Y
has for
source

\mathsf{src}(t)=X
, for
target

\mathsf{tgt}(t)=Y
, for
proof label

\mathsf{lbl}(t)=\theta
, and for
key

\keyop(t)=k
.
Transitions
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
are
coinitial
if
\mathsf{src}(t_{1})=\mathsf{src}(t_{2})
,
cofinal
if
\mathsf{tgt}(t_{1})=\mathsf{tgt}(t_{2})
,
composable
if
\mathsf{tgt}(t_{1})=\mathsf{src}(t_{2})
, and
adjacent
if they are either coinitial, cofinal or composable (in either order).

A
path
is a sequence of transitions
r=t_{1}t_{2}\cdots t_{n}

such that
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
, for
1\leqslant i<n
, are composable.
Its source
\mathsf{src}(r)
is
\mathsf{src}(t_{1})
, its target
\mathsf{tgt}(r)
is
\mathsf{tgt}(t_{n})
, its length
|r|
is
n
, and
its set of keys
\keysop(r)
is
\keyop(t_{1})\cup\cdots\cup\keyop(t_{n})
.
We let
r
and
s
range over paths, and
t
and
u
range over transitions.
A path
r
is
rooted
if
\mathsf{src}(r)
cannot perform a backward transition.

A transition
t_{1}

is connected to
a transition
t_{2}
if there exists a path
r
s.t.
\mathsf{src}(r)=\mathsf{src}(t_{1})
and
\mathsf{tgt}(r)=\mathsf{tgt}(t_{2})
.
Two transitions are
connected
if one is connected to the other.

Definition 6

(Standard and reachable processes)
.

We say that
X
is
standard
and write
\stdop(X)
iff
\keysop(X)=\emptyset
—equivalently, if
X
is a
\mathsf{CCS}
process.
If there exists an
origin process

O_{X}
s.t.
\stdop(O_{X})
and a rooted path
r_{X}:O_{X}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\mkern-5.0mu\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rightarrow$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

is

.

is easily obtained by erasing the keys in

Notation 8
.

The empty path is denoted

\varepsilon
. Given a path
r=t_{1}\cdots t_{n}
, we write
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.7893pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.7893pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.92819pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.53008pt{}}}$\hss}}}r
for its inverse path
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to8.05438pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to8.05438pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.92477pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.72224pt{}}}$\hss}}}t_{n}\cdots\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.09718pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.09718pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.92361pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.72108pt{}}}$\hss}}}t_{1}
.
We also let
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.66312pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.66312pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.77808pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.37321pt{}}}$\hss}}}\varepsilon=\varepsilon
, and
\mathsf{fwd}(t)=t
if
t
is forward,
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.61105pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.61105pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.02078pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to1.81825pt{}}}$\hss}}}t
otherwise.

4
Complementary Relations for Independence and Dependence

This section manipulates three relations defined using only proof keyed labels: an independence relation, a dependence relation, and their
“union”
, which captures connectedness of transitions (
12
).
The important point is that any two proof keyed labels in the connectedness relation are either dependent or independent (
Theorem 14
).
While the dependence relation is inspired by existing works on
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}

1
1

1

In a nutshell,
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
is
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
without keyed prefixes and where choices are discarded if not executed: its definition is provided in
subsection B.1
.

and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}

[
5
,
21
]
, we are not aware of any direct characterisation of dependence and independence that does not postulate their complementarity.
We prove that our independence relation is a conservative extension over the concurrency relation for
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
in
subsection B.1
, and prove
12
and
Theorem 14
in
subsection B.2
.

Definition 9

(Relations on proof labels)
.

Two proof keyed labels
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
are
connected
(resp.
independent
,
dependent
) if
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
(resp.
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
,
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
) can be derived using the rules in
Figure 3
.

Remark 10
.

It is easy to prove that
\mathrel{\iota}
is irreflexive and symmetric, as S
1
is the mirror of S
2
.

Example 11
.

Re-using the labels from
Figure 2
, we have e.g.,

\displaystyle{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{+_{\mathrm{L}}}\overline{a}[m]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\curlyvee}

\displaystyle\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}a[m],{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{+_{\mathrm{L}}}\overline{a}[m]\rangle

\displaystyle\text{ by S${}^{1}$ and A${}^{2}$ for $\mathrel{\curlyvee}$.}

\displaystyle{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{+_{\mathrm{L}}}\overline{a}[m]

\displaystyle\opentimes

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{+_{\mathrm{R}}}b[n]

\displaystyle\text{ by P${}^{1}$ and C${}^{2}$ for $\opentimes$.}

\displaystyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}a[k]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\iota}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{+_{\mathrm{R}}}b[n]

\displaystyle\text{ by P${}^{2}_{k}$ for $\mathrel{\iota}$.}

We first prove that our notion of connectedness of labels is correct w.r.t. to our notion of connected transitions (
5
):

{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}}}}X^{\prime}_{2}
such that
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are connected.

Remark 13
.

Note that the converse of
1
.
does not hold, as e.g.,
a[k]\mathrel{\curlyvee}{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}b[m]
, but
a\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle a[k]$}}}}a[k]
and
\mathbf{0}\mid b\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}b[m]$}}}}\mathbf{0}\mid b[m]
are not connected.
However, e.g.,
t_{1}:a.(\mathbf{0}\mid b)\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle a[k]$}}}}a[k].(\mathbf{0}\mid b)
and
a[k].(\mathbf{0}\mid b)\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}b[m]$}}}}a[k].(\mathbf{0}\mid b[m])
are connected, illustrating
2
.
.

Figure 3:
Relations on proof labels

The notion of connectedness is now leveraged to reduce our search space on proof keyed labels: for example,
{+_{\mathrm{L}}}a[k]
and
{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}b[m]
are neither dependent nor independent, but they also cannot belong to connected transitions (since no process can have both
+
and
\mid
at top level).

Theorem 14

(Complementarity on labels)
.

For all
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
,

1.

If
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
then
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
.

2.

If
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
then
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
.

3.

If
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
then either
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
or
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
, but not both.

The relations
\mathrel{\iota}
and
\opentimes
from
9
are easily extended to
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
’s transitions by using the bijection
{\cdot}^{\circ}={({\cdot}^{\dagger}})^{-1}
from
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
to
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
defined in
Appendix A
.

Definition 15

(Relations on
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
transitions)
.

For transitions
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
in
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
(resp.
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
), we let
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
iff
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are connected and
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1})\mathrel{\iota}\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2})
(resp.
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
iff
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are connected and
\mathsf{lbl}({t_{1}}^{\dagger})\mathrel{\iota}\mathsf{lbl}({t_{2}}^{\dagger})
), and correspondingly for
\opentimes
.

We easily deduce from
Theorem 14
:

Proposition 16

(Complementarity on transitions)
.

If
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are connected then exactly one of
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
and
t_{1}\opentimes t_{2}
holds.

5
Basics of the Axiomatic Approach

We shall now recall the basics of the axiomatic approach
[
31
,
32
]
. Once a model of computation (presented as a labelled transition system with independence
[
41
]
) is reversed, for example a process calculus, it is challenging to prove that it satisfies desired properties such as

causal consistency

[
19
]
or
causal safety
and
causal liveness

[
31
,
32
]
.
The axiomatic approach allows us to obtain such properties—among others—from simpler axioms.
We recall the basic axioms, introduce

polychotomies
and the conditions under which these properties hold (
23
).
We conclude the section by showing that the independence relation for reversible calculi satisfying appropriate axioms is unique (
28
).

Definition 17

(LTSI
[
32
, Defs 2.1–2.3]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{Proc}
be a set of
processes
, ranged over by
P,Q,\ldots
, and
\mathsf{Lab}
a set of
labels
, ranged over by
a,b,\ldots
.

if

to be

.

WF

there is no infinite reverse computation, i.e., there are no
P_{i}
(not necessarily distinct) such that
P_{i+1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle a_{i}$}}P_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 19

(Axiomatic properties and pre-reversible LTSI)
.

Figure 4
presents

the basic axioms

Square property
(SP),
Backward transitions are independent
(BTI),
Well-founded
(WF) and
Propagation of coinitial independence
(PCI)
[
32
, Defs. 3.1, 4.2]
;

other useful properties

Independence of diamonds
(ID),
Independence respects events
(IRE) and
Reversing preserves independence
(RPI).

An LTSI is
pre-reversible
if it satisfies the basic axioms.

SP and BTI are complementary: SP expresses soundness of a definition of independence, such as the one given in
section 4
,
while BTI expresses its completeness.
We save for
subsection 6.1
the proof that
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
fulfill the requirements to leverage the axiomatic approach.

The equivalence classes of transitions, written
[t]
, are the
events
.
We say that an event is
forward
if it is the equivalence class of a forward transition; similarly for reverse events.
Given an event
e=[t]
we let
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.6562pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.6562pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.79405pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.38503pt{}}}$\hss}}}e=[\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.61105pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.61105pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.02078pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to1.81825pt{}}}$\hss}}}t]
.

Definition 21

(Counting events in path
[
32
, Def. 4.11.]
)
.

Let
r
be a path and
e
be an event, we define
\sharp(r,e)
as follows, for
tr
the path made of
t
followed by
r
:

\displaystyle\sharp(\varepsilon,e)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\sharp(tr,e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sharp(r,e)+1&\text{if }t\in e\\
\sharp(r,e)-1&\text{if }t\in\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.6562pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.6562pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.79405pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.38503pt{}}}$\hss}}}e\\
\sharp(r,e)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Definition 22

(Relations on events, transitions
[
32
, Defs 4.14, 4.23, 4.27]
)
.

Two events
e
,
e^{\prime}
are

•

Core independent
2
2

2

Called
“coinitially independent”
in
[
32
]
, but this is confusing when
\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}
is extended to (not necessarily coinitial) transitions.
, written
e\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e^{\prime}
, iff there are coinitial transitions
t
,
t^{\prime}
such that
[t]=e
,
[t^{\prime}]=e^{\prime}
and
t\mathrel{\iota}t^{\prime}
.

Two forward events
e
,
e^{\prime}
are

•

Causally related
, written
e\leq e^{\prime}
, iff for all rooted paths
r
, if
\sharp(r,e^{\prime})>0
then
\sharp(r,e)>0
.

•

In
conflict
, written
e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime}
, iff there is no rooted path
r
such that
\sharp(r,e)>0
and
\sharp(r,e^{\prime})>0
.

We write
e<e^{\prime}
,
e
is a
cause
of
e^{\prime}
, if
e\leq e^{\prime}
and
e\neq e^{\prime}
.
We also extend those relations to transitions by letting
t_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}t_{2}
iff
[t_{1}]\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}[t_{2}]
, and similarly for forward transitions for causal ordering and conflict.

Proposition 23

(Polychotomies
[
32
, Def. 4.28, Proposition 4.29]
)
.

Pre-reversible LTSIs satisfy
polychotomy for forward events
, i.e., for all forward events
e
,
e^{\prime}
, exactly one of the following holds:

1.

e=e^{\prime}
;

2.

e<e^{\prime}
;

3.

e^{\prime}<e
;

4.

e\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime}
; or

5.

e\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e^{\prime}
.

Being defined on events,
<
,
\mathrel{\#}
and
\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}
on transitions are closed under
\sim
, and pre-reversible LTSIs also satisfy
polychotomy for forward transitions
: for all transitions
t
,
t^{\prime}
, exactly one of the following holds:

1.

t\sim t^{\prime}
;

2.

t<t^{\prime}
;

3.

t^{\prime}<t
;

4.

t\mathrel{\#}t^{\prime}
; or

5.

t\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}t^{\prime}
.

Definition 24

(Immediate predecessor)
.

Let
e_{1},e_{2}
be forward events,
e_{1}
is an
immediate predecessor
of
e_{2}
(written
e_{1}\prec e_{2}
) if
e_{1}<e_{2}
and there is no event
e
such that
e_{1}<e<e_{2}
.

Definition 25

(Composable events)
.

Let
e_{1},e_{2}
be events,
e_{1}
is
composable
with
e_{2}
if there are transitions
t_{1}\in e_{1}
and
t_{2}\in e_{2}
such that
t_{1}
is composable with
t_{2}
.

The following result (proved in
Appendix C
) will be used in the proof of
Theorem 43
.

Lemma 26

(Immediate predecessor is not compatible with core independence)
.

Let
e_{1},e_{2}
be forward events in a pre-reversible LTSI satisfying IRE and RPI. Then
e_{1}\prec e_{2}
iff
e_{1}
is composable with
e_{2}
and not
e_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e_{2}
.

We conclude this section by proving that under the mild condition of
“admitting”
pre-reversibility (
27
), any LTSI requiring its independence relation to satisfy SP, BTI and PCI accepts a unique independence relation, and that this relation further uniquely determines the notions of event equivalence, core independence, causal ordering and conflict. Additional material and proofs are in
Appendix C
.

Definition 27

(Admitting pre-reversibility)
.

A combined LTS
(\mathsf{Proc},\mathsf{Lab},\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rightarrow$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}})

admits pre-reversibility
if there exists
\mathrel{\iota}
s.t.
(\mathsf{Proc},\mathsf{Lab},\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rightarrow$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}},\mathrel{\iota})
is a pre-reversible LTSI.

Proposition 28

(Uniqueness)
.

If a combined LTS admits pre-reversibility and we require any independence relation to satisfy SP, BTI and PCI,
then the notions of event equivalence, core independence
\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}
, causal ordering
\leq
and conflict
\#
are uniquely determined.

We are not aware of any previous such uniqueness result.
Its novelty is that, instead of providing a definition of, for example, independence, and then
“manually”
proving that it satisfies various properties, we fix the properties (our basic axioms) and show that there can be only one independence
relation satisfying them, and similarly for the causality and conflict relations.

6
Properties of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}

Properties of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
may be divided into two classes:

1.
those that are derivable from general axioms, which hold for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
by
Theorem 29
below, and

2.
those that depend in some way on the key structure, and do not follow from axioms alone.

6.1
Instantiating the Axiomatic Approach to
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}

We now show that instantiating the axiomatic approach to
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
, where the independence relation
\mathrel{\iota}
is as in

15
, produces
pre-reversible LTSIs that also satisfy IRE and RPI. As a result of
Theorem 29
, the independence relation of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
is

“the only one”
thanks to uniqueness (
28
).

Theorem 29

(The axiomatic approach is applicable to the LTSIs of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
)
.

SP, BTI, WF, PCI, IRE and RPI hold for the LTSIs of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
.

See
subsection D.1
for the proof.
SP, BTI, WF and PCI were already shown for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}

[
5
]
, using as an independence relation the complement of a dependence relation, rather than defining it directly as in this work
3
3

3

The complementarity result from
subsection B.2
makes it
almost
equivalent—the original definition of dependence was missing a case, as we explain in
93
.
Also, we require independent transitions to be connected, whereas in
[
5
]
they had to be coinitial or composable.
.
IRE and RPI were already shown for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
in
[
32
]
.
We can transfer these results from
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
to
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
using their bijection.

We can lift independence and dependence from transitions to events.

Definition 30

(Relations on events)
.

Let
e_{1},e_{2}
be events in the LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
.

1.

e_{1},e_{2}
are
connected
if there are transitions
t_{1}\in e_{1}
and
t_{2}\in e_{2}
such that
t_{1},t_{2}
are connected;

2.

e_{1}\mathrel{\iota}e_{2}
if there are transitions
t_{1}\in e_{1}
and
t_{2}\in e_{2}
such that
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
;

3.

e_{1}\opentimes e_{2}
if there are transitions
t_{1}\in e_{1}
and
t_{2}\in e_{2}
such that
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1})\opentimes\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2})
.

Since the LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
satisfies IRE by
Theorem 29
, if
e_{1}\mathrel{\iota}e_{2}
then
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
for any
t_{1}\in e_{1}
,
t_{2}\in e_{2}
;
similarly for
e_{1}\opentimes e_{2}
using complementarity on connected transitions (
16
).

Remark 31
.

Note that we are not entirely relying on axioms for LTSIs here, since we have defined dependence directly for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
; if we wanted to be purely axiomatic to analyse the LTSI of some other reversible calculus we could instead have
defined
dependence to be the complement of independence.

The next result will be used in the proof of
Theorem 43
.
See
subsection D.2
for the proof.

Lemma 32

(Complementarity for events)
.

Let
e_{1},e_{2}
be connected events in the LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
.

1.

exactly one of
e_{1}\mathrel{\iota}e_{2}
and
e_{1}\opentimes e_{2}
holds;

2.

if
e_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e_{2}
then
e_{1}\mathrel{\iota}e_{2}
;

3.

if
e_{1},e_{2}
are composable and
e_{1}\mathrel{\iota}e_{2}
then
e_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e_{2}
.

The following example shows that the independence relation

\mathrel{\iota}
based on proof labels for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
is more general than core independence
\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}

for non-composable events.

Example 33
.

Consider
(a.b\mid\overline{b}.c)\backslash b
. Executing
a
and then
c
(after the synchronisation on
b
) produces transitions
t_{a}
and
t_{c}
respectively:

\displaystyle t_{a}:(a.b\mid\overline{b}.c)\backslash b

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}a[k]$}}}}(a[k].b\mid\overline{b}.c)\backslash b

\displaystyle t_{c}:(a[k].b[l]\mid\overline{b}[l].c)\backslash b

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}c[n]$}}}}(a[k].b[l]\mid\overline{b}[l].c[n])\backslash b

The labels
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}a[k],{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}c[n]
of
t_{a},t_{c}
respectively are independent by P
{}^{2}_{k}
(since producing
t_{c}
required to have
k\neq n
). However, polychotomy (
23
) yields

[t_{a}]\not\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}[t_{c}]
,
since
c
causally depends on
a
:
[t_{a}]<[t_{c}]
4
4

4

This can also be observed considering that
t_{a}
and
t_{c}
are neither coinitial nor event equivalent to coinitial transitions.
.

6.2
Key-Based Properties of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}

The results in this section depend in some way on the key structure of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
.
A basic design decision in both systems
is that fresh keys are chosen when computing forwards, so that past events will have different keys.
We shall see that we can decide whether transitions belong to the same event simply using keys (
37
). Moreover, it is possible to decide whether one event caused another by purely syntactic means (
Theorem 43
).
Proofs and additional material for this section are in Sections
D.3
and
D.4
(for
Theorem 43
).

Lemma 34

(Independence implies different keys)
.

For both
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
, if
t_{1},t_{2}
are transitions such that
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
, then
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
have different keys.

Lemma 35

(Backward key determinism)
.

For both
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
, if
t_{1},t_{2}
are both backward transitions and
\keyop(t_{1})=\keyop(t_{2})
, then
t_{1}=t_{2}
.

By definition, for any LTSI if
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
then
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
must have the same labels.
However, the converse is false. In
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
consider
t_{1}:a[m].b\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle b[k]$}}}}a[m].b[k]
and
t_{2}:a[n].b\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle b[k]$}}}}a[n].b[k]
. These transitions have the same proof labels, but are not the same event, since they are caused by different events. However, we can show that if
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
or
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
transitions
t_{1},t_{2}
have the same key then
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
, provided that there is a path connecting the target processes which does not use their common key.
The key-based definition of events for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
below is simpler than in the general axiomatic approach
[
32
]
—recalled in
20
—, as well as the earlier definition of
[
35
]
.

Definition 36

(Event key equivalence)
.

Two forward
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
transitions with the same key
t_{1}:X_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}[k]$}}}}X^{\prime}_{1}
and
t_{2}:X_{2}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}[k]$}}}}X^{\prime}_{2}
are
event key equivalent
(
t_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{k}}t_{2}
) if there is a path
r:X^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\mkern-5.0mu\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rightarrow$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}}}}^{*}X^{\prime}_{2}
such that
k\notin\keysop(r)
.
We extend to all transitions (forward or backward) by letting
t_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{k}}t_{2}
iff
\mathsf{fwd}(t_{1})\sim_{\mathsf{k}}\mathsf{fwd}(t_{2})
.
Similarly for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
.

Proposition 37

(Event equivalences coincide)
.

For
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
,
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
iff
t_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{k}}t_{2}
.

Besides being simpler to work with than
20
, a crucial difference between the two definitions is that
36
does not make any use of independence. We could build on this independence-free notion of event to obtain causation and conflict between events (
22
) without using independence.
We can also formulate coinitial independence using keys.
We do this for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
, since the proof labels are not relevant.

Definition 38

(Key independence for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
)
.

1.

Whenever
t:P\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[m]\mkern 2.0mu$}}$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}}Q
,
u:P\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\beta[n]\mkern 2.0mu$}}$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}}R
,

and
t^{\prime}:R\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[m]\mkern 2.0mu$}}$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}}S
, with
m\neq n
then
t,u
are
directly key independent
;

2.

Connected
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
transitions
t,u
are
key independent
if
t\sim_{\mathsf{k}}t^{\prime},u\sim_{\mathsf{k}}u^{\prime}
with
t^{\prime},u^{\prime}
directly key independent.

Key independence corresponds to coinitial independence as defined using the axiomatic approach and independence on proof labels.

Lemma 39

(Coinitial independences coincide)
.

For all coinitial transitions
t,u
in
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
,
t,u
are directly key independent iff
t\mathrel{\iota}u
.

Proposition 40

(Independences coincide)
.

For all transitions
t,u
in
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
,
t,u
are key independent iff
t\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}u
.

Note that since
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
satisfies IRE (
Theorem 29
),

t\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}u
implies
t\mathrel{\iota}u
. The converse does not hold by
33
, adapted from
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
to
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
.
Thus key independence is strictly finer than independence via proof labels.

We conclude this section by showing that the causal ordering on past events (using
22
) can be equivalently computed by a syntactic ordering on keys.

Definition 41

(Partial order on keys
[
30
, Def. 3.1]
)
.

Given a process
X
, its
partial order on
\keysop(X)
, written
\leq_{X}
, is the reflexive and transitive closure of
\ordop(X)
:

\displaystyle\ordop(\mathbf{0})

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\ordop(X+Y)

\displaystyle=\ordop(X)\cup\ordop(Y)

\displaystyle\ordop(\alpha.X)

\displaystyle=\ordop(X)

\displaystyle\ordop(X\mid Y)

\displaystyle=\ordop(X)\cup\ordop(Y)

\displaystyle\ordop(\alpha[n].X)

\displaystyle=\ordop(X)\cup\{n<k\mid k\in\keysop(X)\}

\displaystyle\ordop(X\backslash\lambda)

\displaystyle=\ordop(X)

Definition 42

(Events in a process)
.

We let
\mathbb{E}
be the set of all forward events, and given a reachable process
X
, we let

\evop(X)=\{e\in\mathbb{E}\mid\exists\text{ rooted path }r\text{ to }X,\sharp(r,e)>0\}
.

Theorem 43

(Orderings coincide)
.

For any process
X
, if
e_{1},e_{2}\in\evop(X)
we have:
e_{1}\leq e_{2}
iff
\keyop(e_{1})\leq_{X}\keyop(e_{2})
.

7
KP and DP Bisimulations

Our aim is to define bisimulations for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
which have the same distinguishing power as History Preserving (HP)
bisimulation
[
39
]
5
5

5

Proving that they actually coincide would require us to develop a formal correspondence between behaviour
structures
[
39
]
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
, but bisimulations defined on e.g., automata
[
24
]
, process graphs
[
42
]
or Petri nets
[
44
]
are routinely named HP using informal arguments.

. Since we define them for standard processes, our bisimulations can be seen as for
\mathsf{CCS}
or
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
.
In van Glabbeek and Goltz’s definition of HP bisimulation on configuration structures in
[
43
]
, events of bisimilar structures have
matching labels and there is a bijection between causal orders on events in the matching structures.
Here we show how to define syntactically causal orders and bijections between them using our new results presented so far.

7.1
Pinpointing the Relevant Relations

We have seen in
Theorem 43
that causal order between events of any reachable
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
process can be equally represented by the order among their keys. This gives rise to our first new bisimulation relation called Key-Preserving (KP) bisimulation (
47
).

A question arises if we can use the dependence relation
\opentimes
or the independence relation
\mathrel{\iota}
to capture the causal order between events. We have seen in
33
that some causally dependent connected transitions, which are not composable, have independent labels, so simply using

\opentimes
would not be sufficient to represent
<
. The example below additionally shows that
\mathrel{\opentimes^{*}}
, the transitive closure of
\opentimes
, captures
more than just the causal order. Hence,
\mathrel{\opentimes^{*}}
cannot be used instead of

\leq
on connected forward transitions in a potential characterisation of HP bisimulation.

t
t_{2}
t_{1}
t_{1}^{\prime}
t_{2}^{\prime}
t_{3}^{\prime}
t_{3}
t_{1}^{\prime\prime}

Figure 5:
LTSI for
a.(b\mid c.d)
in
44
.

Example 44
.

In
Figure 5
, letting
t\opentimes t_{1}
,
t\opentimes t_{2}
,
t_{2}\opentimes t_{3}
and
t_{2}^{\prime}\opentimes t_{3}^{\prime}
and all other pairs of transitions have independent labels gives a pre-reversible (and LLG) LTSI.
Clearly
t_{1},t_{2}
are adjacent,
t_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}t_{2}
and
t_{1}\mathrel{\opentimes^{*}}t_{2}
since they depend on
t
and
\opentimes
is symmetric (
10
).
Correspondingly,
t_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}t_{3}
and
t_{1}\mathrel{\opentimes^{*}}t_{3}
but they are not adjacent.

Recall that we have defined the immediate predecessor order
\prec
on events (
24
), which when transitively closed gives us
back the original causal order
<
. This means that
\prec
is a concise representation of our causal order. Noting
that
e_{1}<e_{2}
iff
e_{1}\prec e_{2}
for composable forward events, Lemmas
26
and
32
show that causal order between composed events is precisely represented by dependence of their proof labels:

Lemma 45
.

Let
e_{1},e_{2}
be composable forward events, then
e_{1}<e_{2}
iff
e_{1}\opentimes e_{2}
.

This allows us to define an alternative version of HP bisimulation, called Dependence-Preserving (DP) bisimulation,
where causal order is expressed concisely by dependence
\opentimes
between events and their immediate predecessors.

7.2
Bisimulations

We first define label- and order-preserving bijections:

Definition 46

(Label- and order-preserving bijection)
.

Given
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes
X
,
Y
, a bijection
f:\evop(X)\to\evop(Y)
is
label preserving
if
\forall e\in\evop(X)
,
\lablop(e)=\lablop(f(e))
, with
\lablop(e)
defined as
\lablop(t)
for
t\in e
.
It is
order preserving
if
\keyop(e)\leq_{X}\keyop(e^{\prime})\iff\keyop(f(e))\leq_{Y}\keyop(f(e^{\prime}))
.

We observe that any order-preserving bijection also preserves causal order by
Theorem 43
.

Definition 47

(KP bisimulation (inspired by
[
37
, Sect. 3]
))
.

Let
X
and
Y
be standard
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes.
A relation
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}\subseteq\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\times(\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{E})
is a
Key-Preserving (KP) bisimulation between
X
and
Y
if
(X,Y,\emptyset)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
,

and if whenever
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
, then
f^{\prime}
is a label- and order-preserving bijection from
\evop(X^{\prime})
to
\evop(Y^{\prime})
and

\displaystyle\forall t:X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}X^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow\;

\displaystyle\exists t^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta^{\prime}$}}}}Y^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime}\cup\{[t]\mapsto[t^{\prime}]\})\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}};

(1)

\displaystyle\forall t^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}Y^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow\;

\displaystyle\exists t:X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta^{\prime}$}}}}X^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime}\cup\{[t]\mapsto[t^{\prime}]\})\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}.

(2)

Given any
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes
X,Y
, if there is a KP bisimulation
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}

between
O_{X}
and
O_{Y}
,
such that
(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
for some
f
,
then we say that
X
and
Y
are KP bisimilar, written
X\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}}Y
.

Remark 48
.

If we only consider KP bisimilarity
\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}}
on standard processes (as in
58
), then we can take
the mappings
f
to be the identity on keys, as in FR bisimilarity.

The bijection above is constructed in a step by step fashion starting from a pair of standard processes and the empty mapping, ensuring that it preserves the labels and the order on keys between the matched events. Such a construction provides
KP-grounded triples
(
49
). Note that only action labels and keys are used to define KP bisimulation; the proof part of labels will be used to formulate the next bisimulation.

Definition 49

(KP-grounded triples)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
be a KP bisimulation between standard
X
and
Y
. A triple
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
, is

KP-grounded (for
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
)
if either

X^{\prime}=X
,
Y^{\prime}=Y
and
f=\emptyset
, or if there exists a KP-grounded triple
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime})
such that

(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},f)
was obtained from it using either (
1
) or (
2
) from
47
.

Note that given a KP bisimulation between standard
X,Y
, any triple
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},f)
derived from
(X,Y,\emptyset)
using
47
is KP-grounded for that KP bisimulation, something that will prove useful when studying the corresponding
notion of DP-grounded triples below.

Example 50
.

Consider
a
and
a+a
.
Although their transitions have different proof keyed labels,
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
only matches their labels
(in both cases
a
). As there are no causal dependencies in the executed processes, there is an empty order on keys. There are multiple minimal KP bisimulations for
a
and
a+a
, where label-preserving
f
s are omitted:
for each
m,n,h,k\in\mathsf{K}
we have

\{(a,a+a),(a[m],a[n]+a),(a[h],a+a[k])\}
.
We also note that union of these KP bisimulations (with
f
s omitted), namely
\{(a,a+a)\}\cup\{(a[m],a[n]+a):m,n\in\mathsf{K}\}\cup\{(a[h],a+a[k]):h,k\in\mathsf{K}\}
is also a KP bisimulation for
a
and
a+a
. Overall,
a\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}}a+a
.

Example 51
.

Consider
P=a\mid a
and
Q=a\mid a+a.a
. Although transitions of
P
,
Q
have matching labels and are step bisimilar
[
43
, Def. 7.3]
, they have different causal behaviour. When
Q
executes to
a\mid a+a[l^{\prime}].a[k^{\prime}]
, for some
keys
l^{\prime},k^{\prime}
, it has two causally ordered
a
events with the ordering
l^{\prime}<k^{\prime}
. Executing
P
to
a[l^{\prime}]\mid a[k^{\prime}]
we get
label-preserving bijections between the events of thus executed
P
and
Q
:
\{(a[l],a[l^{\prime}]),(a[k],a[k^{\prime}])\}
for any

l,l^{\prime},k,k^{\prime}
. However, none of such bijections is order preserving because
a[l]
and
a[k]
are
core independent whereas
a[l^{\prime}]
and
a[k^{\prime}]
are causally ordered (or have ordered keys).
Hence,
P\not\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}}Q
.

Example 52
.

Processes
X=a[m].b[n].c
and
Y=b[m].a[n].c
are not KP bisimilar because, although there is an order-preserving mapping
f
with
f(a[m])=b[m]
,
f(b[n])=a[n]
, the mapping is not label preserving. Also,
X
and
Y^{\prime}=b[n].a[m].c
are not KP bisimilar since although there is a label-preserving
g(a[m])=a[n]
,
g(b[n])=b[m]
, it is not order preserving.

Definition 53
.

Given a process
X
, an event
e\in\evop(X)
is
maximal
, if
\forall e^{\prime}\in\evop(X),\neg(e<e^{\prime})
.
We write
\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(X)\right)
for the set of maximal events in
\evop(X)
.

Definition 54

(DP bisimulation (inspired by
[
7
, Sect. 4.3]
))
.

Let
X
and
Y
be standard
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes.
A relation
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}\subseteq\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\times(\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{E})
is a
Dependence-Preserving (DP) bisimulation between
X
and
Y
if

(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
,

and if whenever
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
, then
f^{\prime}
is a label-preserving bijection between
\evop(X^{\prime})
and
\evop(Y^{\prime})
and

\displaystyle\forall t:X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}X^{\prime\prime}\implies

\displaystyle\exists t^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta^{\prime}$}}}}Y^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }\forall e\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(X^{\prime})\right),e\opentimes[t]\iff f^{\prime}(e)\opentimes[t^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\text{ and }(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime}\cup\{[t]\mapsto[t^{\prime}]\})\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}

\displaystyle\forall t^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta^{\prime}$}}}}Y^{\prime\prime}\implies

\displaystyle\exists t:X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}X^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }\forall e\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(Y^{\prime})\right),e\opentimes[t^{\prime}]\iff f^{\prime-1}(e)\opentimes[t]

\displaystyle\text{ and }(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime}\cup\{[t]\mapsto[t^{\prime}]\})\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}

Given any
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes
X
and
Y
, if there exists a DP bisimulation
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
between
O_{X}
and
O_{Y}
and
(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
for some
f
,
then we say that
X
and
Y
are DP bisimilar, written
X\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}}Y
.

In contrast to
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
, the function
f
above is constructed using information about dependence between
proof keyed labels. When the matching transitions
t
,
t^{\prime}
are performed by
X
,
Y
, respectively, where

(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
, we require that, for all maximal events
e,f(e)
in
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
, respectively,
either both
e,[t]
and

f(e),[t^{\prime}]
have dependent proof keyed labels or, by complementarity, both have independent proof keyed labels.

We define
DP-grounded triples
in the corresponding way to that in
49
but using conditions of

54
instead. As a result a mapping in a DP-grounded triple maps a maximal event to a maximal event (as proven in
Appendix E
with
99
).
Note that any triple obtained from standard processes is DP-grounded, and that this allows to discard degenerate cases such as the one illustrated with
57
below.

Example 55
.

Consider
P
,
Q
from
51
and transitions
Q\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{+_{\mathrm{R}}}a[k]$}}}}Q^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{+_{\mathrm{R}}}a[l]$}}}}Q^{\prime\prime}
for some

Q^{\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}
. The matching transitions from
P
are
P\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}a[k^{\prime}]$}}}}P^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}a[l^{\prime}]$}}}}P^{\prime\prime}
, respectively,
for some
P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}
.
Although the transitions of
Q
have dependent labels (
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}a[k]\opentimes{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}a[l]
), the labels of the corresponding
transitions from
P
are independent:
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}a[k^{\prime}]\mathrel{\iota}{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}a[l^{\prime}]
.
Hence,
P\not\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}}Q
.

Example 56
.

Consider
X,Y^{\prime}
from
52
for which we have a label preserving mapping
g
, which can be extended to include

g(c[k])=c[k]
when actions
c
are performed. Moreover, transitions with labels
b[n]
and
c[k]
of
X
have dependent labels, and correspondingly for transitions with labels
a[m]
and
c[k]
of
Y^{\prime}
. However,
X
and
Y^{\prime}
are not DP bisimilar
since we cannot construct a DP bisimulation relation from
O_{X}
and
O_{Y^{\prime}}
, which are standard, because their initial actions do not match.

Example 57
.

Consider non-standard processes
R=a[m].b[n].c[k]+b.a.c
and
S=a.b.c+b[n].a[m].c[k]
which cannot compute forwards.
These are not KP bisimilar because, although there is a label-preserving bijection between their events, the
bijection is not order preserving. However, the origin processes of
R,S
are equal (to
a.b.c+b.a.c
), so are KP bisimilar.
As for DP bisimulation, using the label-preserving mapping on events, we deduce that
R,S
are DP bisimilar. We note that such a mapping cannot be constructed starting from the origin processes of
R,S
using the conditions of
54

(since
a[m]
does not match
b[n]
), and hence is not part of any DP-grounded triple.

57
shows that KP and DP bisimilarity differ on non-standard processes, where bisimilaity triples are not required to be DP-grounded.
Nevertheless, they coincide on standard
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes, which produce only KP- and DP-grounded triples.

Theorem 58
.

Let
P
,
Q
be any standard
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes. Then
P\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}}Q\iff P\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}}Q
.

Example 59
.

Consider the Absorption Law (AL)
[
11
,
13
,
43
]
given below.

(a\mid(b+c))+(a\mid b)+((a+c)\mid b)=(a\mid(b+c))+((a+c)\mid b)

The right hand side process is a subprocess of the left hand side process, so we only check if the transitions
of
a\mid b
can be matched in the right hand side process.
The transition
a
needs to be matched by
a
in
(a+c)\mid b
.
The
b
transition must be matched by
b
in
a\mid(b+c)
. Hence, all the transitions match, hence AL holds for strong bisimulation.
Moreover, we note that there are no composed transitions with dependent labels, so all intermediate maximal events are followed
by (if any) independent events. Hence, AL is valid for KP and DP bisimulations.

Both KP and DP bisimulations are strictly coarser than Hereditary History Preserving (HHP) bisimulation
[
11
,
27
,
34
]

and FR bisimulation
[
36
]
, defined below on
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
, which coincide
[
35
]
.

Definition 60

(FR bisimulation
[
36
, Def. 5.1]
)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes. A relation
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{FR}}}\subseteq\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
is a

between
X
and
Y
if
X\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{FR}}}Y
, and whenever
X^{\prime}\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{FR}}}Y^{\prime}

\displaystyle\forall X^{\prime\prime},X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}X^{\prime\prime}

If there exists an FR bisimulation between
X
and
Y
, we say that
X
and
Y
are FR bisimilar
, written
X\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{FR}}}}Y
.

AL does not hold for FR bisimulation and HHP bisimulation.
Overall, FR bisimulation is strictly finer than KP and DP bisimulations.

Proposition 61
.

Let
P,Q
be any standard
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes. Then
P\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{FR}}}}Q\Rightarrow P\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}}Q
.

8
Conclusion

Our work casts an interesting light not only on the complementarity of dependence and independence, but also on the benefits of the axiomatic approach.
In addition, since KP bisimulations are defined using only keys, they provide a lightweight characterisation of HP for
\mathsf{CCS}
processes that could trigger in turn efficient algorithms
[
25
]
.
Last but not least, our bisimulations generate interesting question about the role of keys in reversibility.
The way they permanently store information allows us to retrieve the causal structure—an element that is lost when constructing a DP bisimulation.
In particular, the status of non-standard processes is of interest: while
a[n]
and
a[m]
are not FR bisimilar (since their keys are different when backtracking), they are KP bisimilar as a mapping preserving the order on keys can be constructed by the bisimulation game starting from their respective origins.
We aim to leverage this to construct an alternative definition of HHP bisimulation, benefiting from our key- or dependence-based techniques, and compare it to HHP bisimulation in
[
34
,
28
]
.

Appendix A
Section
3
: Bijection between
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}

This section defines
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}

[

]

Action, Prefix and Restriction

\stdop(X)

act

\alpha.X\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}\alpha[k].X

k\neq k^{\prime}

pre

X\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\beta[k]$}}X^{\prime}
\alpha[k^{\prime}].X\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\beta[k]$}}\alpha[k^{\prime}].X^{\prime}

\alpha\notin\{\lambda,\overline{\lambda}\}

res

X\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}X^{\prime}
X\backslash\lambda\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}X^{\prime}\backslash\lambda

Parallel
Äpgjy

k\notin\keysop(Y)

{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}

X\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}X^{\prime}
X\mid Y\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}X^{\prime}\mid Y

k\notin\keysop(X)

{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}

Y\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}Y^{\prime}
X\mid Y\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}X\mid Y^{\prime}

syn

X\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\lambda[k]$}}X^{\prime}
Y\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\overline{\lambda}[k]$}}Y^{\prime}
X\mid Y\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\tau[k]$}}X^{\prime}\mid Y^{\prime}

Sum
Äpgjy

\stdop(Y)

{+_{\mathrm{L}}}

X\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}X^{\prime}
X+Y\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}X^{\prime}+Y

\stdop(X)

{+_{\mathrm{R}}}

Y\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}Y^{\prime}
X+Y\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha[k]$}}X+Y^{\prime}

Figure 6:
Forward transition rules for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
. Backward rules are the symmetric versions of the forwards rules, thus are omitted.

Lemma 63
.

For any transition
t
, there exists exactly one derivation whose conclusion is
t
in
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
(resp.
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
).

Proof.

By induction on the label for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
: all the information is stored in the proof (keyed) labels, except for the application of the res and pre rules (or their reverse), but this information can be read off of the structure of the source of the transition. For
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
, it suffices to notice that the source and target of the transition will differ only in the presence or absence of a key, from which the derivation can be uniquely obtained.
∎

Remark 64
.

Note that
63
would not hold as stated if our LTSes were using a structural congruence containing e.g.,
P\mid Q\equiv Q\mid P
, as the processes
P
and
Q
could be swapped any even number of times in the derivations.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}}}}X_{2}
in
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
is unique (
63
), we can use in
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
the rule carrying the same name to obtain a derivation whose conclusion is the desired corresponding transition.

{\cdot}^{\dagger}

is also immediate, and is the inverse of
{\cdot}^{\circ}
.

section 4
: Conservativity and Complementarity of the Independence and Dependence Relations

B.1
Conservativity Over Concurrency of CCS with Proof Labels

Our independence relation is inspired by the concurrency relation
6
6

6

We prefer to avoid the term
“concurrency”
, in general reserved for transitions, but in this section both concurrency and independence describe the same notion.

defined on
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
in
[
15
, Section 3]
.
This section reminds of
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
and of its concurrency relation
7
7

7

The only differences with the aforementioned paper are that it records restrictions in the label, and admits a fixed point operator, but that does not impact our development here.
, defines a mapping between
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
, states and proves the conservativity result over proof labels (

) and over transitions (Corollary
74
and

).

Definition 66

(Proved LTS for
\mathsf{CCS}
)
.

The
proved LTS for
\mathsf{CCS}

(
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
) is
(\mathbb{P},\mathsf{L}^{\mathsf{P}},\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightharpoonup{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}})
where
\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightharpoonup{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}

is given in
Figure 7
.

Note that labels in
Figure 7
are proof labels without keys, over which we quantify using
\theta
also–it will be clear from context if labels are keyed or not.

Action and Restriction

act

\alpha.P\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightharpoonup{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}}}}P

\lablop(\theta)\notin\{\lambda,\overline{\lambda}\}

res

P\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightharpoonup{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}P^{\prime}
P\backslash\lambda\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightharpoonup{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}P^{\prime}\backslash\lambda

Äpgjy

Transition rules with proof labels for
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
.

Definition 67

(Concurrency over proof labels
[
15
, p. 257]
.)
.

The
concurrency relation
\smile
over proof labels
is the least symmetric relation that satisfies

\displaystyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta\smile{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta^{\prime}

(A1)

\displaystyle\theta\smile\theta^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\begin{cases}{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta\smile\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta^{\prime},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta^{\prime\prime}\rangle\\
{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta\smile\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta^{\prime\prime},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta^{\prime}\rangle\end{cases}

(A2)

\displaystyle\theta\smile\theta^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\begin{cases}{\mid_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta\smile{\mid_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta^{\prime}\\
{+_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta\smile{+_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta^{\prime}\\
\end{cases}

(A3)

\displaystyle\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\smile\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}\text{ and }\theta_{\mathrm{R}}\smile\theta_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{\mathrm{L}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta_{\mathrm{R}}\rangle\smile\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}\rangle

(A4)

This relation is irreflexive and symmetric
[
15
, p. 260]
, as is our independence relation
\mathrel{\iota}
(
10
): it suffices to note that S
1
is the mirror version of S
2
.
The relations actually coincide:

Lemma 68

(Conservativity over proof labels)
.

Let
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
be proof labels and
m\neq n
be keys.
Then
\theta_{1}\smile\theta_{2}
iff
\theta_{1}[m]\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}[n]
.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
) The proof is by induction on the length of the derivation of
\theta_{1}\smile\theta_{2}
.
If it is of length
1
, then it is A1, and since
m\neq n
, we have
\theta_{1}[m]\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}[n]
by P
{}^{2}_{k}
.
All the other cases amount to mapping A2 to S
1
, A3 to P
1
or C
1
depending on the operator considered, and A4 to S
3
.
No rule is mapped to S
2
, but its presence is needed to obtain closure by symmetry of
\mathrel{\iota}
, which is assumed for
\smile
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Immediate by re-using the previous mapping, since A1 relaxes the condition on key in P
{}^{2}_{k}
. If S
2
is used, then the symmetric closure of
\smile
allows us to conclude.
∎

As with
\mathrel{\iota}
we can define when transitions are concurrent (
73
), and show that a very tight correspondence between independent
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
transitions and concurrent
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
transitions.
However, this requires first to define mappings between their transitions, which in turn requires the following definitions and lemma:

Definition 69

(Key removal from proof keyed labels)
.

We define
\krop:\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{L}^{\mathsf{P}}
as:

\displaystyle\krop(\upsilon\alpha[k])

\displaystyle=\upsilon\alpha

\displaystyle\krop(\upsilon\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\upsilon_{1}\lambda[k],{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\upsilon_{2}\overline{\lambda}[k]\rangle)

\displaystyle=\upsilon\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\upsilon_{1}\lambda,{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\upsilon_{2}\overline{\lambda}\rangle

Definition 70

(Pruning function
[
36
, Def. 5.20]
)
.

We define
\prunop:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{P}
as:

\displaystyle\prunop(\mathbf{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\prunop(X\mid Y)

\displaystyle=\prunop(X)\mid\prunop(Y)

\displaystyle\prunop(\alpha.X)

\displaystyle=\alpha.\prunop(X)

\displaystyle\prunop(\alpha[k].X)

\displaystyle=\prunop(X)

\displaystyle\prunop(X\backslash\lambda)

\displaystyle=\prunop(X)\backslash\lambda

\displaystyle\prunop(X+Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\prunop(X)&If $\stdop(Y)$ but $\stdop(X)$ does not hold\\
\prunop(Y)&If $\stdop(X)$ but $\stdop(Y)$ does not hold\\
\prunop(X)+\prunop(Y)&Otherwise\\
\end{cases}

As for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
(
63
),
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
enjoys unique derivations:

Lemma 71
.

For any transition
t
, there exists exactly one derivation whose conclusion is
t
in
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the label: all the information is stored in the proof labels, except for the application of the res and pre rules, but this information can be read off of the structure of the source of the transition.
∎

Definition 72

(Mapping between
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
)
.

We define the
key forgetful
(
{\cdot}_{\keysop}^{\circ}
) and
key enrichment
(
{\cdot}_{\keysop}^{\dagger}
) mappings

\displaystyle{\cdot}_{\keysop}^{\circ}

\displaystyle:(X_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}X_{2})\mapsto(\prunop(X_{1})\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightharpoonup{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\krop(\theta)$}}}}\prunop(X_{2}))

(
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
to
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
)

\displaystyle{\cdot}_{\keysop}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:(P_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightharpoonup{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}P_{2},k)\mapsto(P_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta[k]$}}}}X_{2})

s.t.
\prunop(X_{2})=P_{2}

(
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
to
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
)

essentially as in
65
, additionally leveraging
71
.

Note that the key enrichment function has to be given a key as an extra parameter, to avoid having to pick a fresh one.
Deterministic strategies to select keys have been explored
[
9
]
and could be leveraged to ensure that
{\cdot}_{\keysop}^{\circ}
and
{\cdot}_{\keysop}^{\dagger}
are inverses, but this would complicate our definitions while not improving our results presented below.

Definition 73

(Concurrent transitions)
.

Two transitions
t_{0}:P\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightharpoonup{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}P_{0}
,
t_{1}:P\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightharpoonup{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}}}Q_{1}
are
concurrent
, denoted
t_{0}\smile t_{1}
, if and only if
\theta_{0}\smile\theta_{1}
.

Finally, we have two immediate corollaries of
68
:

Corollary 74

(
\mathrel{\iota}
extends
\smile
)
.

Given two
\mathsf{CCS}^{\mathsf{P}}
transitions
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
and two keys
m\neq n
,
t_{0}\smile t_{1}\implies{(t_{0},m)}_{\keysop}^{\dagger}\mathrel{\iota}{(t_{1},n)}_{\keysop}^{\dagger}
.

Corollary 75

(
\mathrel{\iota}
is conservative over
\smile
)
.

Given two
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
transitions
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
,
t_{0}\mathrel{\iota}t_{1}\implies{(t_{0})}_{\keysop}^{\circ}\smile{(t_{1})}_{\keysop}^{\circ}
.
