Proposition 77
.

For every proof label
\theta
, there exists a process that realises it, and we denote it
\real{\theta}
.

Proof.

We prove it by induction on the size of
\theta
:

\theta=\alpha[k]

Then
\alpha.\mathbf{0}
realises
\theta
.

\theta={+_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta^{\prime}

By induction hypothesis,
\real{\theta^{\prime}}
realises
\theta^{\prime}
, and
\mathbf{0}+\real{\theta^{\prime}}
or
\real{\theta^{\prime}}+\mathbf{0}
, depending on the value of
\mathrm{d}
, will realise
\theta
.

\theta={\mid_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta^{\prime}

By induction hypothesis,
\real{\theta^{\prime}}
realises
\theta^{\prime}
, and
\mathbf{0}\mid\real{\theta^{\prime}}
or
\real{\theta^{\prime}}\mid\mathbf{0}
, depending on the value of
\mathrm{d}
, will realise
\theta
.

\theta=\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta_{2}\rangle

By induction hypothesis,
\real{\theta_{1}}
(resp.
\real{\theta_{2}}
) realises
\theta_{1}
(resp.
\theta_{2}
), so
\real{\theta_{1}}\mid\real{\theta_{2}}
realises
\theta
. ∎

Lemma 78
.

For all reachable processes
X
and
Y
, there exists a path
X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\mkern-5.0mu\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rightarrow$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}}}}^{*}Y
iff
O_{X}=O_{Y}
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow

Informally, the key argument is that
X
and
Y
will diverge only in the name, presence or absence of keys, and that erasing them (e.g., using the
\tostdop
function
[
30
, p. 128]
) will give the same standard process, which will be the origin of both.

\Leftarrow

It suffices to consider the path
X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\mkern-5.0mu\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rightarrow$\cr\raise 2.29996pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Proposition 80
.

For all
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
, if a derivation of
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
exists, then it is unique.

Proof.

This follows easily by induction on the structure of
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
, since no two conclusions overlap in the definition of the connectivity relation (
Figure 3
).
∎

See
12

Proof.

We prove each equation separately, letting
\mathrm{d}
,
\mathrm{p}
range over the
directions

\mathrm{L}
(eft) and
\mathrm{R}
(ight).

(
1
)

Since
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are connected, we have by
79
that
O_{X_{1}}=O_{X_{2}}=P
, and we reason by induction on
P
:

P=\mathbf{0}

Then no transitions are possible and this case is vacuously true.

P=\alpha.Q

Then there are three cases:

•

If
X_{1}=P
and
\theta_{1}
is of the form
\alpha[k]
, then by A
1
we have
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
.

•

If
X_{2}=P
and
\theta_{2}
is of the form
\alpha[k]
and
\theta_{1}
is not of the form
\beta[k^{\prime}]
for some
\beta
and
k^{\prime}
, then by A
2
we have
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
.

•

that are connected.
We have that

by

, and it is immediate to observe that either

(depending on the value of

and

S

This case is nearly identical to the previous one.

Then

Proposition 81
.

If
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
then neither
\theta_{1}
nor
\theta_{2}
is of the form
\alpha[k]
.

Proof.

This follows easily once observed that the only base case for
\mathrel{\iota}
in
Figure 3
, P
{}^{2}_{k}
, requires
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
to be prefixed by
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}
and
{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}
, respectively.
∎

Finally, we have all the elements to prove that the independence and dependence relations partition the connectedness relation:

See
14

Proof.

1.

Any proof of
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
can be transformed into a proof of
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
by systematically replacing rules one-by-one.
The only noticeable difference is that the condition on keys in P
{}^{2}_{k}
is absent in P
2
, but since we are relaxing a condition, this does not go in the way of the proof transformation.

2.

Similarly, any proof of
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
can be transformed into a proof of
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
by systematically replacing rules one-by-one, and relaxing the condition on keys in P
{}^{2}_{k}
.
Note that the premises in S
3
involves
\opentimes

and

\mathrel{\curlyvee}
, hence requiring to transform only one of the two derivation sub-trees.

3.

We prove this by induction on the length of
the
proof of
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
, unique by
80
:

Length
1

Then, the proof of
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
is one of the following:

A
1
, A
2
or C
2

Then, we can immediately obtain a proof of
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
using the same rule.
By
81
, we know that no proof of
\alpha[k]\mathrel{\iota}\theta
nor of
\theta\mathrel{\iota}\alpha[k]
exist, and by inspection of the rules of
\mathrel{\iota}
, we can observe that no proof of
+_{\mathrm{d}}\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}+_{\overline{\mathrm{d}}}\theta_{2}
can exist.

P
2

Then, there are two cases:

\kayop(\theta)=\kayop(\theta^{\prime})

In this case, we can obtain a proof of
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
using P
{}^{2}_{k}
, but cannot derive
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
since P
{}^{2}_{k}
cannot be used and no other rule has a conclusion of the form
{\mid_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta\mathrel{\iota}{\mid_{\overline{\mathrm{d}}}}\theta^{\prime}
.

\kayop(\theta_{1})\neq\kayop(\theta_{2})

In this case, we can obtain a proof of
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
using P
{}^{2}_{k}
, but cannot derive
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
since P
{}^{2}_{k}
cannot be used and no other rule has a conclusion of the form
{\mid_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta\opentimes{\mid_{\overline{\mathrm{d}}}}\theta^{\prime}
.

Length
>1

Then, the proof of
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{2}
terminates with one of the following rules:

C
1

Then
\theta_{1}={+_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}
,
\theta_{2}={+_{\mathrm{d}}}\theta_{2}^{\prime}
, and by the induction hypothesis there exists a proof of
\theta_{1}^{\prime}\opentimes\theta_{2}^{\prime}
or of
\theta_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}^{\prime}
, but not of both.
In both cases, we obtain the desired result by applying C
1
to the proof obtained by induction, and no proof exists for the other relation since C
1
is the only rule with a conclusion of this shape.

P
1
, S
1
and S
2

Those cases are similar to the previous one.

S
3

Then,
\theta_{1}=\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{\mathrm{L}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta_{\mathrm{R}}\rangle
,
\theta_{2}=\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}\rangle
, and by induction, we have one of those cases:

\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
and
\theta_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}

Then applying S
3
gives that
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
, and no proof of
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
can exist since the only rule that could be applied to obtain this conclusion is S
3
but its premises cannot be proven by induction.

\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
and
\theta_{\mathrm{R}}\opentimes\theta_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}

Since
\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
, then
\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
by (1) of the current proposition, and
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
can be proven using S
3
.
By induction, no proof of
\theta_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}
exists, and hence no proof of
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
can exist since the only rule that could be applied to obtain this conclusion is S
3
but its premises cannot be proven.

\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\opentimes\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
and
\theta_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}

This case is identical to the previous one.

\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\opentimes\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
and
\theta_{\mathrm{R}}\opentimes\theta_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}

Then
\theta_{1}\opentimes\theta_{2}
can be obtained using S
3
, since
\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\opentimes\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
implies
\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{\curlyvee}\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
by (2) of the current proposition.
Since, by induction, neither
\theta_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
nor
\theta_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}
can be proven, it is clear that
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
cannot be proven either.∎

Appendix C
section 5
: Additional Material and Proofs

C.1
Proof of
26

See
26

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Suppose that
e_{1}\prec e_{2}
.

We start by showing that
e_{1}
is composable with
e_{2}
.
Let
r
be a forward-only rooted path ending with a forward transition
t_{2}\in e_{2}
.
Since
e_{1}<e_{2}
we must have a forward transition
t_{1}\in e_{1}
occurring before
t_{2}
in
r
.
Thus
r=r^{\prime}t_{1}st_{2}
for some
r^{\prime},s
.
We proceed by induction on
|s|
.

If
|s|=0
then
e_{1}
is composable with
e_{2}
as required.
So suppose
|s|>0
.
Clearly for any
t
in
s
we cannot have
t_{1}<t<t_{2}
.
So transitions
t
of
s
fall into two groups:

1.

t_{1}<t\not<t_{2}

2.

t_{1}\not<t

If a member
t
of group 1 is immediately before a member
t^{\prime}
of group 2, then since
t_{1}\not<t^{\prime}
we have
t\not<t^{\prime}
using transitivity of
<
. Using polychotomy (
23
) we deduce
t\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}t^{\prime}
, so that
t\mathrel{\iota}t^{\prime}
using IRE. Using RPI and SP, the two transitions can be swapped.
Thus if group 1 is non-empty then the last group 1 transition
t
can be moved to immediately before
t_{2}
. Since
t\not<t_{2}
, the two transitions can be swapped and
|s|
reduces.

So suppose that group 1 is empty. But then the first transition
t
of
s
is in group 2, so that
t_{1}\not<t
. In this case
t_{1}
and
t
can be swapped, again reducing
|s|
.
Hence
e_{1}
is composable with
e_{2}
.

Now we show not
e_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e_{2}
. Since
e_{1}\prec e_{2}
,
e_{1}<e_{2}
and hence
e_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e_{2}
cannot hold by polychotomy.

(
\Leftarrow
)
Suppose that
e_{1}
is composable with
e_{2}
and not
e_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e_{2}
.
Let
r=st_{1}t_{2}
be a rooted forward-only path with
t_{1}\in e_{1}
and
t_{2}\in e_{2}
.
We can use polychotomy to deduce that
e_{1}<e_{2}
.
Suppose forward event
e
is such that
e_{1}<e<e_{2}
.
Since
\sharp(r,e_{2})=1
, we must have
\sharp(r,e)=1
. But then there is
t\in e
such that
t
occurs in
r^{\prime}
. This contradicts
e_{1}<e
. Hence
e_{1}\prec e_{2}
.
∎

Proposition 84

(Uniqueness of coinitial independence)
.

Suppose that
(\mathsf{Proc},\mathsf{Lab},\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rightarrow$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}},\mathrel{\iota}_{1})
and
(\mathsf{Proc},\mathsf{Lab},\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rightarrow$\cr\raise 2.35104pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\hbox to1.94pt{\vbox to3.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45161pt\lower-1.81996pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -0.16 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.08437pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 0.12 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.85 2.24 C -1.51 0.9 -0.76 0.26 0 0 C -0.76 -0.26 -1.51 -0.9 -1.85 -2.24}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mkern 4.0mu$}\crcr}},\mathrel{\iota}_{2})
are two pre-reversible LTSIs with the same underlying combined LTS. Then
\mathrel{\iota}_{1}
and
\mathrel{\iota}_{2}
agree on coinitial transitions.

Proof.

Suppose that
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are coinitial transitions with
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}_{1}t_{2}
.
By SP for
\mathrel{\iota}_{1}
we get a square.
This is non-degenerate (all states are distinct) by
82
.
We deduce that
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}_{2}t_{2}
using ID which holds by
83
.
By symmetry we deduce the result.
∎

See
28

Proof.

The independence relation
\mathrel{\iota}
is determined for coinitial transitions
by
84
. From
20
, the equivalence on transitions
\sim
is determined by
\mathrel{\iota}
on coinitial transitions. Furthermore, it is clear from
22
that
\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}
,
\leq

and
\mathrel{\#}
are then uniquely determined.
∎

Definition 86

we have
t\mathrel{\iota}u
iff

are connected and
I(\mathsf{und}(\alpha),\mathsf{und}(\beta))
.

Proposition 87
.

If an LTSI is LLG then it satisfies PCI, IRE and RPI.

Proof.

As the proof of
[
32
, Prop. 6.12]
, given that PCI, IRE and RPI preserve connectedness of transitions.
∎

Proposition 88
.

The LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
is LLG.

Proof.

Immediate from
15
.
∎

Proposition 89
.

The LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
satisfies PCI, IRE and RPI.

Proof.

Since the LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
is LLG by
89
, it satisfies PCI, IRE and RPI by
87
.
This was essentially already observed in
[
32
, Sect. 6.2]
, using a slightly different definition of label-generated.
∎

To transfer axioms from
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
to
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
we use the bijection
{\cdot}^{\circ}={({\cdot}^{\dagger}})^{-1}
of
65
.

Proposition 90
.

The LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
satisfies SP, BTI and WF.

Proof.

This follows essentially from
85
:

SP

Let
t
and
u
be coinitial
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
transitions such that
t\mathrel{\iota}u
. Then
{t}^{\dagger}
and
{u}^{\dagger}
are coinitial transitions in
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
with
{t}^{\dagger}\mathrel{\iota}{u}^{\dagger}
. By SP for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
there exist cofinal transitions
u^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
, from which we obtain the desired cofinal transitions
{u^{\prime}}^{\circ}
and
{t^{\prime}}^{\circ}
.

BTI

Given coinitial backward transitions
t
,
t^{\prime}
, BTI for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
gives
{t}^{\dagger}\mathrel{\iota}{t^{\prime}}^{\dagger}
, which implies
t\mathrel{\iota}t^{\prime}
.

WF

The absence of infinite reverse computation follows from the finite number of keys in processes. ∎

The event equivalences obtained when instantiating
20
to
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
are preserved by their isomorphism.

Proposition 91
.

For all
t,u
in
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
(resp.
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
)

\displaystyle t\sim u\implies{t}^{\dagger}\sim{u}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\text{(resp.{}~ }t\sim u\implies{t}^{\circ}\sim{u}^{\circ}\text{)}

Proof.

Being on opposite sides of a diamond as in
20
is trivially preserved by
{\cdot}^{\dagger}
and
{\cdot}^{\circ}
, and so is
\mathrel{\iota}
by
15
.
∎

Proposition 92
.

The LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
satisfies PCI, IRE and RPI.

Proof.

By transferring the axioms from
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
. Note that we cannot use
87
, since the LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
is not LLG.

For PCI, we use mappings
{\cdot}^{\dagger}
and
{\cdot}^{\circ}
, much as for the proof of SP (
90
).

For IRE, suppose
t\sim t^{\prime}\mathrel{\iota}u
. Then
{t}^{\dagger}\sim{t^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\mathrel{\iota}{u}^{\dagger}
, using
91
. By IRE for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
we get
{t}^{\dagger}\mathrel{\iota}{u}^{\dagger}
, implying
t\mathrel{\iota}u
as required.

For RPI, we use mappings
{\cdot}^{\dagger}
and
{\cdot}^{\circ}
, much as for PCI.
∎

We finally have all the elements in place to prove
Theorem 29
:

Proof of
Theorem 29
.

By Propositions
85
,
89
,
90
and
92
.
∎

D.1.1
Proof of
85

We now provide a complete proof of
85
.
As in the first papers that proved this result
[
4
,
5
]
, the main challenge is to prove SP.

Remark 93

(Differences with original proof)
.

Our proof below of SP and BTI have some differences with the original proof. Indeed, independence (originally called
“concurrency”

[
5
, Deﬁnition 10]
) was
defined
by complementarity, instead of being
proved
complementary, as we do now with
Theorem 14
.
Providing a direct definition solves a minor problem with the original definition, which considered e.g.,

\displaystyle a\mid b

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}a[m]$}}}}a[m]\mid b

and

\displaystyle a\mid b

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}b[m]$}}}}a\mid b[m]

independent, even if there cannot be cofinal transitions to
a[m]\mid b[m]
, hence violating SP.
This adjustment primarily impacts the proof of BTI and of Equations
3
–
5
, presented below.

However, the proof of SP, presented p.
D.1.1
, requires the same three main ingredients:

1.

The following three implications
[
5
, Lemmas 7–9]
,

\displaystyle X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}X_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}Y\text{ with }\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}

\displaystyle\implies\exists X_{2},X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}X_{2}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}Y

We use the exact same definition and proof of
item 3
.

Proof of
Equation 3
.

In short, the proof proceeds by induction on the length of the deduction for the derivation of
X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}X_{1}
, using
Equation 18
to enable the induction hypothesis if
\theta_{1}
is not a prefix.
The proof requires a particular care when
X
is not standard, more particularly if the last rule is pre, but
item 3
provides just what is needed to deal with this case.

The proof proceeds by induction on the length of the deduction for the derivation of
X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}X_{1}
.

Length
1

In this case, the derivation is a single application of act, and
\theta_{1}
is of the form
\alpha[k]
.
But
\alpha[k]\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
cannot hold by
81
, so this case is vacuously true.

Length
>1

We proceed by case on the last rule.

pre

There exists
\alpha
,
k
,
X^{\prime}
and
X_{1}^{\prime}
s.t.
X=\alpha[k].X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}\alpha[k].X_{1}^{\prime}=X_{1}
and
\kayop(\theta_{1})\neq k
.
As
\alpha[k].X_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}Y
we know that
\kayop(\theta_{2})\neq k

[
30
, Lemma 3.4]
.
Furthermore, since
k
occurs attached to
\alpha
in
X_{1}
and since
X_{1}
makes a
forward
transition to reach
Y
,
k\notin\keysop(\rem^{\alpha}_{k}(X_{1}))\cup\keysop(\rem^{\alpha}_{k}(Y))
.
Hence, we can apply
item 3
from left to right twice to obtain

\rem^{\alpha}_{k}(\alpha[k].X^{\prime})=X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}\rem^{\alpha}_{k}(\alpha[k].X^{\prime}_{1})=X^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}\rem^{\alpha}_{k}(Y)

As
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
by hypothesis, we can use the induction hypothesis to obtain that there exists
X_{2}
s.t.
X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}X_{2}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}\rem^{\alpha}_{k}(Y)
.
Since
\kayop(\theta_{2})\neq k
, we can append pre to the derivation of
X^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}X_{2}
to obtain
\alpha[k].X^{\prime}=X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}\alpha[k].X_{2}
.
Using
item 3
again, but from right to left, we obtain that
\rem^{\alpha}_{k}(\alpha[k].X_{2})=X_{2}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}\rem^{\alpha}_{k}(Y)
implies
\alpha[k].X_{2}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}Y
, which concludes this case.

res

This is immediate by induction hypothesis.

\mathbf{{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}}

There exists
X_{\mathrm{L}}
,
X_{\mathrm{R}}
,
\theta_{1}^{\prime}
,
X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}
, and
Y_{\mathrm{L}}
,
Y_{\mathrm{R}}
s.t.
X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}X_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}Y
is

X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}\text{.}

Then,
\pi_{\mathrm{L}}(X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}})=X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}
and the proof proceeds by case on
\theta_{2}
:

\theta_{2}
is
{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta_{2}^{\prime}

Then
X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{R}}
,
X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}=Y_{\mathrm{L}}
and the occurrences of the rules
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}
and
{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}
can be swapped to obtain

X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}\text{.}

\theta_{2}
is
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{2}^{\prime}

Then,
X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}
and
X_{\mathrm{R}}=Y_{\mathrm{R}}
.
As
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}=\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}={\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{2}^{\prime}
, it is the case that
\theta_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}^{\prime}
in
X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}
by
item 3
, and we can use the induction hypothesis to obtain
X_{2}
s.t.
X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{2}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}
, from which it is immediate to obtain
X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{2}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}=Y_{\mathrm{L}}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}
.

\theta_{2}
is
\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle

Since
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}=\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}=\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle
, we have that
\theta_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\iota}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}}
in
X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}
by
item 3
.
Hence, we can use the induction hypothesis to obtain
X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}$}}}}X_{2}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}
.
Since we also have that
X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{R}}
, we can compose both paths using first syn, then
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}
to obtain

X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle$}}}}X_{2}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}\text{.}

{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}

This is symmetric to
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}
.

syn

There exists
X_{\mathrm{L}}
,
X_{\mathrm{R}}
,
{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}}
,
{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}}
,
X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}
,
X_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}
,
Y_{\mathrm{L}}
and
Y_{\mathrm{R}}
s.t.
X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}X_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}Y
is

X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mid X_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}\text{.}

Then,

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{L}}(X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mid X_{1_{\mathrm{R}}})

\displaystyle=X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{R}}(X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mid X_{1_{\mathrm{R}}})

\displaystyle=X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}

and the proof proceeds by case on
\theta_{2}
:

\theta_{2}
is
{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}

Then
X_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{R}}
,
X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}=Y_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle\mathrel{\iota}{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}
. Then by
Equation 18

X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{R}}
and
{\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}}\mathrel{\iota}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}
.
We can then use the induction hypothesis to obtain
X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}X_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{R}}
from which it is immediate to obtain

X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}$}}}}X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}=Y_{\mathrm{L}}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}\text{.}

\theta_{2}
is
{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}}

This is symmetric to
{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}
.

\theta_{2}
is
\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle

This case is essentially a combination of the two previous cases.
Since
\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle=\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}=\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle
,
Equation 18
gives the two paths

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{L}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}X_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{R}}

and
{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{\iota}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}}
and
{\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}}\mathrel{\iota}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}
, respectively.
By induction hypothesis, we obtain two paths

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}$}}}}X_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}X_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{R}}

that we can then combine using syn twice to obtain, as desired,

X_{\mathrm{L}}\mid X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle$}}}}X_{2_{\mathrm{L}}}\mid X_{2_{\mathrm{R}}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\langle{\mid_{\mathrm{L}}}{\theta_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}},{\mid_{\mathrm{R}}}{\theta_{1_{\mathrm{R}}}}\rangle$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}\mid Y_{\mathrm{R}}\text{.}

{+_{\mathrm{L}}}

There exists
X_{\mathrm{L}}
,
X_{\mathrm{R}}
,
\theta_{1}^{\prime}
,
\theta_{2}^{\prime}
,
{X_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}}
, and
Y_{\mathrm{L}}
s.t.
X\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}$}}}}X_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}$}}}}Y
is

X_{\mathrm{L}}+X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{+_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}{X_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}}+X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{+_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}+X_{\mathrm{R}}\text{.}

All transitions happen on the left side and
X_{\mathrm{R}}
remains unchanged as otherwise we could not sum two non-standard terms, so that
\theta_{2}
must be of the form
{+_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{2}^{\prime}
.
Then, we can use
Equation 18
to obtain

X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}{X_{1}}_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}

and
\theta_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}^{\prime}
.
Hence we can use the induction hypothesis to obtain
X_{2}
s.t.
X_{\mathrm{L}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{2}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}
.
From this, it is easy to obtain

X_{\mathrm{L}}+X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{+_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{2}^{\prime}$}}}}X_{2}+X_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{+_{\mathrm{L}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}$}}}}Y_{\mathrm{L}}+X_{\mathrm{R}}=Y_{\mathrm{L}}+Y_{\mathrm{R}}\text{.}

{+_{\mathrm{R}}}

This is symmetric to
{+_{\mathrm{L}}}
.∎

.

is when summand operands are involved, i.e., if the deduction for

Proof of
85
.

We can now prove that the LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
satisfies SP, BTI and WF:

SP

The proof is by case on the directions of the transitions, and always follows the same pattern: use
7
to orient the transitions to meet the premises of Equation
3
,
4
or
5
, use the appropriate equation to obtain new paths, and finally use again
7
to orient them as desired.

\kayop(\theta_{1})\neq\kayop(\theta_{2})
: by a simple inspection of the backward rules in

X
can perform two different backward transitions, then their labels must have different keys.

act

\alpha[k]

and
\stdop(X^{\prime})

Hence,
X
cannot perform two different transitions, and this case is vacuously true.

D.2
Proof of
32

See
32

Proof.

1.

By IRE (
89
) and complementarity (
Theorem 14
).

2.

By IRE.

3.

Suppose
e_{1},e_{2}
are composable and
e_{1}\mathrel{\iota}e_{2}
. By IRE we have composable
t_{1}\in e_{1}
and
t_{2}\in e_{2}
such that
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
. By RPI (
89
),
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.09718pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.09718pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.92361pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.72108pt{}}}$\hss}}}t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
.
Since
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.09718pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.09718pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.92361pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.72108pt{}}}$\hss}}}t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are coinitial,
e_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e_{2}
as required.
∎

D.3
subsection 6.2
: Proofs of Lemmas and Propositions

See
34

Proof.

From the rules for
\mathrel{\iota}
we see that if
\theta_{1}\mathrel{\iota}\theta_{2}
then
\keyop(\theta_{1})\neq\keyop(\theta_{2})
. Hence for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
, if
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
then
\keyop(t_{1})\neq\keyop(t_{2})
.
For
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
, using
{\cdot}^{\dagger}
is as in
65
, if
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t_{2}
then
{t_{1}}^{\dagger}\mathrel{\iota}{t_{2}}^{\dagger}
, so that
\keyop({t_{1}}^{\dagger})\neq\keyop({t_{2}}^{\dagger})
and so
\keyop(t_{1})\neq\keyop(t_{2})
.

∎

See
35

Proof.

For
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
, suppose that
t_{1}:X\mathrel{\hbox to24.45pt{\vbox to9.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.22337pt\lower-4.79166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

See
37

Proof.

We present the proof for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
; it works equally well for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Suppose that
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
.
It is enough to consider a single diamond. But then the targets of
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are joined by
t
such that
t_{1}\mathrel{\iota}t
. Then
t_{1}
and
t
have different keys by
34
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
Suppose that we have transitions
t_{1}:X_{1}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{1}[k]$}}}}X^{\prime}_{1}
and
t_{2}:X_{2}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}[k]$}}}}X^{\prime}_{2}
with
t_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{k}}t_{2}
.
We proceed by induction on the length of the path from
X^{\prime}_{1}
to
X^{\prime}_{2}
.
If the path is of length zero then we have
t_{1}=t_{2}
by backward key determinism (
35
).

If the path has non-zero length, by
94

we can convert the path into a parabolic path without increasing length, and without introducing new labels, and so without introducing new keys. Hence it is still the case that
k
does not occur in the parabolic path.
By the parabolic property, either the first transition is backward or the last transition is forward. We consider the case where the last transition is forward; the other case is similar.

So suppose that the last transition in the path is
t:Y^{\prime}\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta[k^{\prime}]$}}}}X^{\prime}_{2}
, where
k^{\prime}\neq k
.
By BTI we have
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.61105pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.61105pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.02078pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to1.81825pt{}}}$\hss}}}t\mathrel{\iota}\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.09718pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.09718pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.92361pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.72108pt{}}}$\hss}}}t_{2}
. We then use SP to complete a diamond with transitions
t^{\prime}_{2}:Y\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta_{2}[k]$}}}}Y^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}:Y\mathrel{{\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle\theta[k^{\prime}]$}}}}X_{2}
for some
Y
.
By PCI
t^{\prime}_{2}\mathrel{\iota}t^{\prime}
, and so
t^{\prime}_{2}\sim t_{2}
.
By induction hypothesis
t_{1}\sim t^{\prime}_{2}
.
Hence
t_{1}\sim t_{2}
.
∎

D.4
Proof of
Theorem 43

Proving this last result requires some additional lemmas and definitions.

We need a strengthened version of the Parabolic Lemma (PL): in addition to the statement in
[
32
, Prop. 3.4]
, it states that the new
“parabolic”
path should introduce no new events compared to the old path.

Lemma 94

(Strengthened PL)
.

In an LTSI satisfying SP and BTI, for any path
r
there are forward-only paths
s,s^{\prime}
such that
r\approx\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.68744pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.68744pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.77426pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to2.35066pt{}}}$\hss}}}ss^{\prime}
and
|s|+|s^{\prime}|\leq|r|
.
Moreover, if
t
in
s
or
t
in
s^{\prime}
then
t\sim t^{\prime}
for some
t^{\prime}
in
r
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as for
[
32
, Prop. 3.4]
, where it follows from axioms SP and BTI. We just note that any new transitions in
s
or
s^{\prime}
are obtained by replacing
t\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.72452pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.72452pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.728pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to3.23033pt{}}}$\hss}}}u
(for some forward transitions
t,u
) by
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.53008pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to7.53008pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.43288pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to5.93521pt{}}}$\hss}}}u^{\prime}t^{\prime}
where
t^{\prime}\sim t
and
u^{\prime}\sim u
.
∎

Lemma 95

(Order from events to keys)
.

Suppose
X
is reachable, and suppose that
e_{1},e_{2}\in\evop(X)
are such that
e_{1}\prec e_{2}
.
Then
(\keyop(e_{1}),\keyop(e_{2}))\in\ordop(X)
.

Proof.

We apply Lemmas
26
and
32
as well as complementarity (
Theorem 14
) to deduce that
e_{1}
is composable with
e_{2}
and
e_{1}\opentimes e_{2}
.
Let
k_{1}=\keyop(e_{1})
,
k_{2}=\keyop(e_{2})
.
Composability of
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
implies that
k_{1}\neq k_{2}
.
Let
rt_{1}t_{2}
be a forward-only path with
t_{1}\in e_{1}
,
t_{2}\in e_{2}
.
We have
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1})\opentimes\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2})
. We refer to
Figure 3
for rules for
\opentimes
and
\mathrel{\iota}
.

By structural induction on
X
.
There are various cases:

P
.

This case cannot arise, since
\evop(P)=\emptyset
.

{\alpha[k].X}
.

There are various sub-cases:

1.

k_{1}=k
.
Then
k_{2}\in\keysop(X)
, and so
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(\alpha[k].X)
.

2.

k_{2}=k
.
This cannot arise, since
t_{1}
occurs before
t_{2}
in the path
rt_{1}t_{2}
.

3.

k_{1},k_{2}\neq k
.
Then
k_{1},k_{2}\in\keysop(X)
.
There is a forward-only path
r^{\prime}t^{\prime}_{1}t^{\prime}_{2}
obtained by omitting the first transition of
r
and removing prefixes
\alpha[k]
from processes.
Clearly
\mathsf{lbl}(t^{\prime}_{1})\opentimes\mathsf{lbl}(t^{\prime}_{2})
.
By induction we have
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
, and so
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(\alpha[k].X)
.

X+Q
.

Then there is a forward-only path
r^{\mathrm{L}}t_{1}^{\mathrm{L}}t_{2}^{\mathrm{L}}
obtained by projecting onto the left-hand component.
We use rule C
1
to deduce
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1}^{\mathrm{L}})\opentimes\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2}^{\mathrm{L}})
.
So
\evop_{X}(k_{1})\opentimes\evop_{X}(k_{2})
and these events are composable.
By induction we have
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
, and so
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X+Q)
.

P+X
.

Similar to the preceding case.

X\backslash\lambda
.

Then there is a forward-only path
r^{\prime}t^{\prime}_{1}t^{\prime}_{2}
obtained by removing the restriction.
Clearly
\evop_{X}(k_{1})\opentimes\evop_{X}(k_{2})
and these events are composable.
By induction we have
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
, and so
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X\backslash\lambda)
.

X\mid Y
.

There are various sub-cases:

1.

k_{1},k_{2}\in\keysop(X)\cap\keysop(Y)
.
Then there are forward-only paths
r^{\mathrm{d}}t_{1}^{\mathrm{d}}t_{2}^{\mathrm{d}}
(
\mathrm{d}\in\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{R}\}
) obtained by projecting onto the left-hand and right-hand components.
We use rule S
3
to deduce
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1}^{\mathrm{d}})\opentimes\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2}^{\mathrm{d}})
for
\mathrm{d}\in\{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{R}\}
.
Wlog suppose
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1}^{\mathrm{L}})\opentimes\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2}^{\mathrm{L}})
.
By induction we have
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
, and so
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X\mid Y)
.

2.

k_{1}\in\keysop(X)\cap\keysop(Y)
and
k_{2}\in\keysop(X)\setminus\keysop(Y)
.
Then there is a forward-only path
r^{\mathrm{L}}t_{1}^{\mathrm{L}}t_{2}^{\mathrm{L}}
obtained by projecting onto the left-hand component.
We use rule S
2
to deduce
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1}^{\mathrm{L}})\opentimes\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2}^{\mathrm{L}})
.
By induction we have
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
, and so
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X\mid Y)
.

3.

k_{1}\in\keysop(X)\cap\keysop(Y)
and
k_{2}\in\keysop(Y)\setminus\keysop(X)
.
Similar to the preceding case.

4.

k_{1}\in\keysop(X)\setminus\keysop(Y)
and
k_{2}\in\keysop(X)\cap\keysop(Y)
.
Then there is a forward-only path
r^{\mathrm{L}}t_{1}^{\mathrm{L}}t_{2}^{\mathrm{L}}
obtained by projecting onto the left-hand component.
We use rule S
1
to deduce
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1}^{\mathrm{L}})\opentimes\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2}^{\mathrm{L}})
.
By induction we have
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
, and so
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X\mid Y)
.

5.

k_{1}\in\keysop(Y)\setminus\keysop(X)
and
k_{2}\in\keysop(X)\cap\keysop(Y)
.
Similar to the preceding case.

6.

k_{1},k_{2}\in\keysop(X)\setminus\keysop(Y)
.
Then there is a forward-only path
r^{\mathrm{L}}t_{1}^{\mathrm{L}}t_{2}^{\mathrm{L}}
obtained by projecting onto the left-hand component.
We use rule P
1
to deduce
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1}^{\mathrm{L}})\opentimes\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2}^{\mathrm{L}})
.
By induction we have
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
, and so
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X\mid Y)
.

7.

k_{1},k_{2}\in\keysop(Y)\setminus\keysop(X)
.
Similar to the preceding case.

8.

k_{1}\in\keysop(X)\setminus\keysop(Y)
and
k_{2}\in\keysop(Y)\setminus\keysop(X)
.
This case cannot arise, since we have
\mathsf{lbl}(t_{1})\mathrel{\iota}\mathsf{lbl}(t_{2})
by rule P
{}^{2}_{k}
.

9.

k_{1}\in\keysop(Y)\setminus\keysop(X)
and
k_{2}\in\keysop(X)\setminus\keysop(Y)
.
Similar to the preceding case.
∎

Lemma 96

(Event keys properties)
.

For any reachable
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}
process
X
:

1.

if
e_{1},e_{2}\in\evop(X)
then
\keyop(e_{1})\neq\keyop(e_{2})
;

2.

\{\keyop(e)\mid e\in\evop(X)\}=\keysop(X)
.

Proof.

1.

We can consider a forward-only rooted path, which must exist by PL (
94
). Clearly all keys of transitions must be distinct.

2.

By structural induction. The most interesting case is parallel composition.
A rooted path with target
X\mid Y
can be
“projected”
9
9

9

Here and below, the informal term
“project”
is to be read in the technical sense of the
\pi_{\mathrm{d}}
and
\rho_{\mathrm{d}}
functions discussed on p.
2
., around item
2
.
.
into rooted paths with targets
X
and
Y
respectively. ∎

In view of
96
we can make the following definition.

Definition 97

(Event key)
.

Let
X
be reachable and let
k\in\keysop(X)
. Define
\evop_{X}(k)
to be the unique event
e\in\evop(X)
such that
\keyop(e)=k
.

The events of
\evop(X)
are partially ordered as in
22
.
Recall the ordering on keys generated from
\ordop(X)
from
41
.

Lemma 98

(Order from keys to events)
.

Suppose
X
is reachable and
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
. Then
\evop_{X}(k_{1})<\evop_{X}(k_{2})
.

Proof.

By structural induction on
X
.
In the definition of
e_{1}<e_{2}
we can restrict to forward-only paths using
[
32
, Lemma 4.26]
, since the LTSI of
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
is pre-reversible.
The proof is best done using proved transitions, since this helps to clarify parallel composition.
It is convenient to use event equivalence
\sim_{\mathsf{k}}
as in
36
, which is equivalent to
\sim
as in
20
by
37
.

There are various cases:

P
.

This case cannot arise, since
\keysop(P)=\emptyset
.

{\alpha[k].X}
.

There are two sub-cases:

1.

Suppose that
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(\alpha[k].X)
, and this is derived from
k_{1}=k
and
k_{2}\in\keysop(X)
.
Suppose that
r
is any rooted forward-only path with
\sharp(r,\evop_{\alpha[k].X}(k_{2}))=1
.
Then the first transition of
r
is labelled with
\alpha[k]
, so that
\sharp(r,\evop_{\alpha[k].X}(k_{1}))=1
also.
This shows that
\evop_{\alpha[k].X}(k_{1})<\evop_{\alpha[k].X}(k_{2})
.

2.

Suppose that
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(\alpha[k].X)
, and this is derived from
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
.
Suppose also that
\evop_{\alpha[k].X}(k_{1})\not<\evop_{\alpha[k].X}(k_{2})
.
Then there is a rooted forward-only path
r
with
\sharp(r,\evop_{\alpha[k].X}(k_{2}))=1
and
\sharp(r,\evop_{\alpha[k].X}(k_{1}))=0
.
Suppose that
t
is the transition in
r
which belongs to
\evop_{\alpha[k].X}(k_{2})
. Then
t\sim_{\mathsf{k}}t^{\prime}
where
t^{\prime}
belongs to some rooted forward-only path
r^{\prime}
with target
\alpha[k].X
.
Let
s
be a path not containing
k_{2}
from
\mathsf{tgt}(t)
to
\mathsf{tgt}(t^{\prime})
.

We can omit the initial
\alpha[k]
transition in
r
and
r^{\prime}
, and delete all
\alpha[k]
prefixes in
r,r^{\prime},s
, yielding paths
r_{0},r^{\prime}_{0},s_{0}
and transitions
t_{0}\in r_{0}
and
t^{\prime}\in r^{\prime}_{0}
. Using
s_{0}
we see that
t_{0}\sim_{\mathsf{k}}t^{\prime}_{0}
. Using
r^{\prime}_{0}
we see that
t^{\prime}_{0}\in\evop_{X}(k_{2})
, and so
t_{0}\in\evop_{X}(k_{2})
.
Now
\sharp(r,\evop_{X}(k_{2}))=1
and
\sharp(r,\evop_{X}(k_{1}))=0
,
showing that
\evop_{X}(k_{1})\not<\evop_{X}(k_{2})
, contradicting the induction hypothesis.

X+Q

Similar to sub-case
2
.
for
\alpha[k].X
.

P+X

Similar to the preceding case.

P\backslash\lambda

Similar to sub-case
2
.
for
\alpha[k].X
.

X\mid Y

Suppose that
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X\mid Y)
, and this is derived from
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X)
.
Suppose also that
\evop_{X\mid Y}(k_{1})\not<\evop_{X\mid Y}(k_{2})
.
Then there is a rooted forward-only path
r
with
\sharp(r,\evop_{X\mid Y}(k_{2}))=1
and
\sharp(r,\evop_{X\mid Y}(k_{1}))=0
.
Suppose that
t
is the transition in
r
which belongs to
\evop_{X\mid Y}(k_{2})
. Then
t\sim_{\mathsf{k}}t^{\prime}
where
t^{\prime}
belongs to some rooted forward-only path
r^{\prime}
with target
X\mid Y
.
Let
s
be a path not containing
k_{2}
from
\mathsf{tgt}(t)
to
\mathsf{tgt}(t^{\prime})
.

We can project
r,r^{\prime},s
onto their left-hand components (by omitting any moves made solely on the right-hand component), yielding paths
r_{\mathrm{L}},r^{\prime}_{\mathrm{L}},s_{\mathrm{L}}
and transitions
t_{\mathrm{L}}\in r_{\mathrm{L}}
and
t^{\prime}\in r^{\prime}_{\mathrm{L}}
. Using
s_{\mathrm{L}}
we see that
t_{\mathrm{L}}\sim_{\mathsf{k}}t^{\prime}_{\mathrm{L}}
. Using
r^{\prime}_{\mathrm{L}}
we see that
t^{\prime}_{\mathrm{L}}\in\evop_{X}(k_{2})
, and so
t_{\mathrm{L}}\in\evop_{X}(k_{2})
.
Now
\sharp(r,\evop_{X}(k_{2}))=1
and
\sharp(r,\evop_{X}(k_{1}))=0
,
showing that
\evop_{X}(k_{1})\not<\evop_{X}(k_{2})
, contradicting the induction hypothesis.

The case where
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(X\mid Y)
is derived from
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\ordop(Y)
is similar.
∎

Appendix E
section 7
: Proofs and Additional Results

The goal of this appendix section is to prove
58
, which only applies to standard
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes. This means that,
especially in the case of DP bisimulation, it is important to distinguish between arbitrary triples and DP-grounded triples. Thus we work here with KP-grounded and DP-grounded triples.

Since we consider DP bisimulation only on standard processes, we have that maximal events get mapped to maximal events by label-preserving bijections of DP-grounded triples:

Lemma 99
.

Let
(X,Y,f)
be DP-grounded for some
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
. Then
\forall e\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(X)\right)
,
f(e)\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(Y)\right)
.

Proof.

We proceed by a proof by contradiction and suppose that
\exists e\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(X)\right)
such that
f(e)\notin\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(Y)\right)
.
Then, wlog, there exists
f(e^{\prime})\in\evop(Y)
(since
f
is a label-preserving bijection between
\evop(X)
and
\evop(Y)
) such that
f(e)\prec f(e^{\prime})
.
Then by
26
,
f(e)
is composable with
f(e^{\prime})
and by
45

we get
f(e)\opentimes f(e^{\prime})
.

Now, observe that
(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}\iff(Y,X,f^{-1})\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
by definition.
Hence, just before
f(e^{\prime})
was triggered,
f(e)
was maximal (this would otherwise contradict
f(e)\prec f(e^{\prime})
), and the transition associated to
f(e^{\prime})
was a forward transition (since the triple is grounded) such that

f(e)\opentimes f(e^{\prime})\iff f^{-1}(f(e))\opentimes f^{-1}(f(e^{\prime}))

Since we have already established that
f(e)\opentimes f(e^{\prime})
, then it must be the case that
e\opentimes e^{\prime}
.

Since
e,e^{\prime}
are events of
X
we have
e\not\mathrel{\#}e^{\prime}
. Also,
e\not\mathrel{\mathsf{ci}}e^{\prime}
follows from
32
since the
events have dependent labels.
Moreover,
e\neq e^{\prime}
since
f
is a bijection. The two remaining options are
e<e^{\prime}
and

e^{\prime}<e
by polychotomy.
The first contradicts
e
being maximal. Consider
e^{\prime}<e
. When
(e^{\prime},f(e^{\prime}))
was added to
f
, then

(f^{-1}(f(e)),f(e))
already must have been in
f
since
f(e)\prec f(e^{\prime})
. This means there is a path where

f^{-1}(f(e))=e
precedes
e^{\prime}
contradicting
e^{\prime}<e
. Hence the result.
∎

See
58

Proof.

Assume any standard
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
processes
P
and
Q
. We shall work with KP-grounded and DP-grounded
tuples, namely with
(X,Y,f)
, where
X,Y
are the derivatives of
P,Q
, respectively, obtained by applying either
47
or
54
, and
f
is an appropriate bijection between

X
and
Y
. It implies that if
(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
then
f
is label- and order-preserving bijection,
and
f
is label-preserving in
(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
.

When proving each of the implications, we shall consider only cases for transitions of
X
since the cases for
transitions of
Y
are proved correspondingly.

\Rightarrow

Assume
P\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}}Q
. By
47
there exists
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
between
P
and
Q

such that
(P,Q,\emptyset)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
, and there are KP-grounded triples
(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
for all the appropriate
X,Y
and label- and order-preserving
f
.
Hence, we similarly assume that
(P,Q,\emptyset)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
for some
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
.
We then need to prove that, given a KP-grounded triple
(X,Y,f)
, for each pair
([t],[t^{\prime}])
of events mapped by
f
as in
47
,
\forall e\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(X)\right),e\opentimes[t]\iff f(e)\opentimes[t^{\prime}]
:

\Rightarrow

Suppose
e\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(X)\right)
and
e\opentimes[t]
. Since
t
is a transition of
X
and
e
is a maximal event of
X
,
we have that
e
and
[t]
are connected. They are also composable since assuming the opposite would imply
that
e
is not maximal in
\evop(X)
. Then
e<[t]
by
45
,
and
\keyop(e)<_{X^{\prime}}\keyop([t])
by
Theorem 43
.
Since
f
is order preserving, we get
\keyop(f(e))<_{Y^{\prime}}\keyop([t^{\prime}])
for
[t^{\prime}]=f([t])
.
We have
f(e),[t^{\prime}]\in\evop(Y^{\prime})
, so by applying
Theorem 43

we get
f(e)<[t^{\prime}]
. By
99
,
f(e)
is a maximal in
Y
and using
the same argument as in
99
, we show that
f(e),[t^{\prime}]
are composable.
Hence
f(e)\opentimes[t^{\prime}]
by
45
.

\Leftarrow

The argument is very much like in the
\Rightarrow
case using
99

and the fact that
f
is order-preserving bijection.

\Leftarrow

Assume
P\mathrel{\sim_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}}Q
. By
54
, there is
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
between
P
and
Q
such that

(P,Q,\emptyset)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
, and there are DP-grounded triples
(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
for all the appropriate
X,Y

and label-preserving
f
. So we similarly assume that

(P,Q,\emptyset)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
for some
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{KP}}}
.

Next we prove that
f
of any DP-grounded triple for
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
, namely constructed from
\emptyset

by adding pairs
([t],[t^{\prime}])
for the matching transitions of
P,Q
and
their respective derivatives according to conditions of
54
, is order preserving. Assume

(X,Y,f)\in\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
for some label-preserving
f
. We show each implication in
46
separately.

\Rightarrow

Consider
e\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(X)\right)
which is composed with
[t]
, and
e\opentimes[t]
holding.
Assume for contradiction that
\keyop(e)<_{X}\keyop([t])
and
\keyop(f(e))\not<_{Y}\keyop([t^{\prime}])
,
using the notations of
47
. We get
e<[t]
by applying
Theorem 43
.
By
54
we get
[t^{\prime}]
and by applying
99
we also get

f(e)\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(Y)\right)
and
f(e)\opentimes[t^{\prime}]
. Since
f(e)
and
[t^{\prime}]
are composable
(shown as in the proof of
99
), we
obtain
f(e)<[t^{\prime}]
by
45
, and
\keyop(f(e))<_{Y}\keyop([t^{\prime}])
by

Theorem 43
: contradiction.
Hence,
\keyop(e)<_{X}\keyop([t])
implies
\keyop(f(e))<_{Y}\keyop([t^{\prime}])
.

\Leftarrow

Consider
f(e)\in\mathopen{\mathrm{max}}\left(\evop(X)\right)
and
[t^{\prime}]
with
f(e)\opentimes[t^{\prime}]
holding.
Assume for contradiction

\keyop(f(e))<_{Y}\keyop([t^{\prime}])
and
\keyop(e)\not<_{X}\keyop([t])
using the notations of
47
.
The last implies

e\not<[t]
by
Theorem 43
. Since
e,[t]
are composable we get

e\not\opentimes[t]
(
45
). However, since
f(e)\opentimes[t^{\prime}]
holds we obtain by
54

that
e\opentimes[t]
holds: contradiction.

This means that
f
of any DP-grounded triple for
\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{DP}}}
is order preserving. ∎

Proving the last result requires to define the
“No repeated events”
(NRE) axiom
[
32
, Def. 4.18]
, which holds for pre-reversible LTSes
[
32
, Prop. 4.21]
:

For any rooted path
r
and any event
e
we have
\sharp(r,e)\leq 1

(NRE)

See
61

Proof.

FR bisimulation matches forward transitions of
P
,
Q
by equating their labels and keys (and does the same to reverse
transitions), whereas KP bisimulation only looks at labels of forward transitions.
Moreover, in addition to preserving labels of matched transitions (and events) of
P
,
Q
FR bisimulation preserves causal order on events
[
35
, Proposition 5.6]
provided that
NRE holds. Since the LTSIs for
\mathsf{CCS}{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathsf{P}}
is pre-reversible, NRE holds.
Finally, since any bijection that preserves causal order
is also order preserving (
Theorem 43
) we get the result.
∎
